                                               KAKEYA AND FURSTENBERG PROBLEMS FOR STICKY SETS OF TUBES

                                                                                   HONG WANG, DMITRII ZAKHAROV


                                                    Abstract. We study incidence problems for sticky sets of tubes. In the first part, we prove a sharp
                                                    Furstenberg set estimate for sticky sets of tubes in R3 satisfying convex Wolff axioms. In the second
                                                    part, we prove the Kakeya set conjecture in R4 for sticky sets of tubes satisfying convex Wolff axioms.
arXiv:2609.22035v1 [math.CA] 18 Sep 2026

                                           1. Introduction
    A Kakeya set is a compact set in Rd which contains a unit length line segment in every direction.
The Kakeya set conjecture is the assertion that any such set must have full Hausdorff dimension. The
two dimensional case was proved by Davies [4], see [11, 34] for an introduction and survey of the Kakeya
set problem. A Furstenberg set is a generalization of the Kakeya set where instead of containing line
segments, the set is required to intersect with many lines in a set of fixed dimension. Furstenberg sets
were introduced in Furstenberg’s unpublished work and in Wolff’s influential exposition [33]. On the
discretized level, these are questions about how much can collections of thin tubes overlap with each
other and what geometric structure occurs if they do.
   A sharp Furtenberg set estimate in the plane was proven in 2023 by Ren and Wang [25] and the
Kakeya set conjecture in R3 was resolved in 2025 by Wang and Zahl [31] (see also surveys by Guth
[5], [6], [7], and a streamlined proof by Guth, Wang and Zahl [9]). In both cases, the proof proceeds
by reducing the problem to a more structured subproblem, namely when the set of tubes is ‘sticky’,
and then solving this subproblem. The sticky Furstenberg estimate in the plane was proven earlier
by Orponen and Shmerkin using their ABC sum-product theorem [21]. The sticky case of the 3-
dimensional Kakeya conjecure was resolved by Wang and Zahl [32] by following a strategy proposed
by Katz and Tao [27].
   In higher dimensions, some partial progress has been made [10, 13, 14] but sharp results are still a
major challenge. In this paper, we study sticky versions of the Fustenberg and Kakeya problems in
dimensions three and four, respectively. The following two theorems are the main results of this paper.
We refer to Section 3 for standard definitions and notation.
                   KAKEYA AND FURSTENBERG PROBLEMS FOR STICKY SETS OF TUBES                                   3

Theorem 1.1. Let s ∈ (0, 1], t ∈ (0, 2]. For any ϵ > 0 there exists η(s, t, ϵ) > 0 such that the following
holds for δ sufficiently small. Let T be a (2t, δ −η )-AD-regular set of δ-tubes in R3 and satisfies the
Convex Wolff axiom Ct−CW (T) ⩽ δ −η . Suppose Y is a (δ, s, δ −η )-shading on T such that
                                   X
                                       |Y (T )|δ ⩾ δ η |T| max |Y (T )|δ .
                                                                        T ∈T
                                       T ∈T

Then
                                     [
(1)                                         Y (T )       ⩾ δ ϵ |Y (T )|δ δ − min(2t,t+s,2) .
                                     T ∈T            δ

   The convex Wolff axiom means that every convex set U contains at most |U |t |T| many tubes from
T. For most values of s, t, our proof goes through with a somewhat weaker version of the Convex Wolff
axiom while giving the same lower bound (1), see Section 6.1 for details.
   This resolves the sticky case of the three dimensional Furstenberg set conjecture, see [29, Conjecture
0.13]. In [29], they posed this conjecture as an intermediate step towards the two-ends Furstenberg
estimate in R3 [29, Conjecture 0.9], which has since been disproven by Cohen [2], and the Fourier
restriction conjecture [29, Conjecture 0.1].
   Standard examples show that the exponent of δ in (1) is optimal (see [29]). The               −2t cor-
                                                                                 S exponent δ
responds to the ‘sparse’ case when shadings Y (T ) are essentially disjoint and | Y (T )|S≈ |Y (T )||T| ≈
|Y (T )|δ −2t . The exponent δ −2 corresponds to the ‘dense’ case when the union E = Y (T ) satisfies
|T ∩ E| ≈ |T ||E| for all T ∈ T. The exponent δ −s−t corresponds to the most interesting example where
E is a δ-neighborhood of a 3-dimensional grid and T consists of tubes with rational slopes and offsets.
More precisely, pick some N, r ⩾ 1 and let P = [−1, 1]3 ∩ N −1 Z3 be the N × N × N grid. Let L be
the set of lines ℓ of the form
               ℓ = p + R(a, b, c),     p ∈ [−1/2, 1/2]3 ∩ N −1 Z3 ,               a, b, c ∈ Z ∩ [−N/r, N/r]
Every such line satisfies |ℓ ∩ P | ≳ r and moreover the intersection ℓ ∩ P is an arithmetic progression of
diameter ∼ 1. For each line ℓ ∈ L there are ∼ r-many ways to select the ‘central’ point p, so by double
counting we have
                                            r|L| ∼ N 3 (N/r)3 ,
i.e. |L| ∼ N 6 r−4 . Let T be the union of δ-tubes of the form T = Nδ ∩ [−1, 1]3 over all lines ℓ ∈ L and
for each such T ∈ T define the shading Y (T ) = Nδ (ℓ ∩ P ). To get an example for the Furstenberg set
problem, we need to arrange parameters so that T is a (δ, 2t)-set and Y (T ) is a (δ, s)-set. This forces
the choice
                                        δ −2t ∼ N 6 r−4 , δ −s ∼ r.
For these parameters, we then have
                  [
                     Y (T ) = |P | = N 3 = (N 6 r−4 )1/2 r2 ∼ δ −t−2s ∼ |Y (T )|δ δ −t−s ,
                              δ

matching the middle term in (1). Finally, it is not hard to verify that this example satisfies the required
Convex Wolff axiom. Note this set of tubes is not sticky, so it’s technically not an example for Theorem
1.1. However one can modify this construction to make it sticky, see [19].
Theorem 1.2. For any ϵ > 0 there exists η(ϵ) > 0 such that the following holds for δ sufficiently small.
Let T be a (3, δ −η )-AD-regular set of δ-tubes in R4 and satisfies the Convex Wolff axiom CCW (T) ⩽ δ −η .
Suppose Y is a δ η -dense shading on T. Then
                                                         [
                                                                Y (T ) ⩾ δ ϵ .
                                                         T ∈T
4                                    HONG WANG, DMITRII ZAKHAROV

   This theorem is a direct generalization of the sticky Kakeya estimate in R3 [30, Theorem 5.2]. An
interesting feature of Theorem 1.2 is that we only impose the Convex Wolff axioms on T instead of a
more general polynomial Wolff axioms. The reason is that for sticky sets of tubes, the Convex Wolff
axiom (tubes do not concentrate in convex sets) automatically implies a corresponding Polynomial
Wolff axiom (tubes do not concentrate in bounded complexity semi-algebraic sets). On the other
hand, it is well-known that for general sets of tubes in dimension at least 4, Convex Wolff axioms are
not sufficient: there exists a collection T of ≈ δ −3 -many δ-tubes in R4 that satisfies the convex Wolff
axiom CCW (T) ≲ 1 and at the same time T is contained in a union of δ −1/2 -many δ-neighborhoods of
quadratic surfaces. So, in particular, | Y (T )| ≲ δ 1/2 . See Section 9 in [5] for more details.
                                         S
   Our Theorem 1.2 circumvents this issue of tubes concentrating in neighborhoods of quadrics. It
appears that in order to resolve the full Kakeya conjecture in R4 , one would need to deal with this
difficulty is some way. While our result does not address this important issue, we hope that our results
and the techniques developed in this paper will be useful for the general case.
   We also hope that our techniques will be useful in higher dimensional sticky problems. In fact, some
parts of the proof of Theorem 1.2 address some special cases of sticky Kakeya in Rd . On the other
hand, new ideas seem to be required to deal with other cases. We leave this direction for future work.
Paper organization. In Section 2 we give an overview of the proofs. Sections 3–5 contain some
preliminary results used for both main theorems. Theorem 1.1 is proven in Part I (Sections 6–16) and
Theorem 1.2 is proven in Part II (Sections 17–22). The two parts are mostly self-contained and do not
require readers familiarity with the contents of each other.
Prior work. Let us mention some earlier work studying sticky                        in higher dimensions.
                                                                    S Kakeya sets −0.603...·d
Kulkarni [15, Proposition 31] proved an estimate of the form | T Y (T )|δ ⪆ δ                 , where T is a
(d − 1)-dimensional sticky set of tubes in Rd (not required     to satisfy any Wolff axioms).    For large d
this improves on the general volume lower bound | T T |δ ⪆ δ −0.597...·d due to Katz–Tao [12] for sets
                                                      S

of tubes in Rd with δ-separated directions.
   In dimension 4, Choudhuri [1] recently showed that sticky Kakeya sets have Hausdorff dimension at
least 3.25, improving on the lower bound 3.059 for general Kakeya sets due to Katz–Zahl [14]. These
estimates utilize the planebrush estimate from [14] (for sets of tubes where tubes through a fixed point
span a 2-plane) and a trilinear estimate of Guth–Zahl [10] (for sets of tubes where tubes through a
fixed point span a 3-plane). There are some parallels to these approaches in our work, but ultimately
the proofs are quite different. We refer to the survey [34] for other recent work on the Kakeya problem.

                                          2. Proof overview.
2.1. Sticky Kakeya in 3D. Proofs of Theorems 1.1 and 1.2 follow a similar general route and share
several important ideas. To illustrate the overall approach, it is convenient to start with a review of
the proof of the sticky Kakeya conjecture in R3 . We will then discuss modifications needed in the
Furstenberg setting and in the higher dimensions.
   The sticky Kakeya theorem in R3 is the following statement proved by Wang–Zahl in [32], [30].
Theorem 2.1. For any ϵ > 0 the following holds for all sufficiently small η, δ. Let T be a (2, δ −η )-
AD-regular set of δ-tubes           the Convex Wolff axiom with error δ −η and let Y be a δ η -dense
                      S satisfying −3+ϵ
shading on T. Then | T Y (T )|δ ⩾ δ     .
                    KAKEYA AND FURSTENBERG PROBLEMS FOR STICKY SETS OF TUBES                                     5

   Let us briefly introduce the notation that we use in this section. We are intentionally slightly
informal here and postpone rigorous definitions to Section 3.
      • For a set U let T[U ] = {T ∈ T : T ⊂ U }.
      • For T ∈ T and ϱ ∈ [δ, 1] let Tϱ = Nϱ T be a ϱ-tube containing T (there is at most O(1) such
        distinct ϱ-tubes).
      • (2, δ −η )-AD-regular means that |T[Tϱ ]| ∈ [δ η (ϱ/δ)2 , δ −η (ϱ/δ)2 ] for all ϱ ∈ [δ, 1] and T ∈ T. In
        [32] this property is referred to as sticky. In the following discussion, we refer to this property
        as almost AD-regular.
      • Convex Wolff axiom with error δ −η means that |T[U ]| ⩽ δ −η |U ||T| for any convex set U ⊂ R3 .
      • δ η -dense shading YSon T is a collection of sets Y (T ) ⊂ T with T ∈T |Y (T )|δ ⩾ δ η |T|δ −1 . We
                                                                          P
        denote U (T, Y ) = T ∈T Y (T ).
                    P
                        |Y (T )|
   Write µ(T) = |UT(T,Y )| for the multiplicity of (T, Y ), i.e. the average number of tubes passing
through a point p ∈ U (T, Y ). By assumption and double counting, we have µ(T) ≈ δ −κ . Suppose that
κ is the largest possible constant for which there are sticky Kakeya configurations with multiplicity
δ −κ for arbitrarily small δ. For the sake of contradiction, assume that κ > 0.

2.1.1. Initial reduction. In the first step of the proof of Theorem 2.1 we use rescaling and thickening
operations to reduce the problem to a more structured case. Suppose that the statement is false, i.e.
for some constant κ > 0 (think of κ = 0.1) and any η > 0 and arbitrarily small δ there is a configuration
of tubes (T, Y ) as in Theorem 2.1 with error δ −η and |U (T, Y )|δ ⪅ δ κ−3 . The error δ −η is not essential
for the following discussion, so we will subsume it in the ‘≈’ notation.
   The properties of the sticky Kakeya configuration (T, Y ) are preserved under the following two
operations.
Thickening. For ϱ ∈ (δ, 1) consider
                                  S the set of essentially distinct ϱ-tubes Tϱ covering T and define a
shading on Tϱ by Y (Tϱ ) = Nϱ ( T ∈T[Tϱ ] Y (T )). This gives a new sticky Kakeya configuration (Tϱ , Yϱ )
on scale ϱ, i.e. Tϱ is a collection of ϱ-tubes that is almost AD-regular and satisfies the Convex Wolff
axiom.
Rescaling. For some ϱ ∈ (δ, 1) fix a ϱ-tube Tϱ ∈ Tϱ and consider an affine linear map ψTϱ : R3 → R3
such that ψTϱ (Tϱ ) ≈ B1 (0). Consider the rescaled set of tubes
                                         TTϱ = {ψTϱ (T ), T ∈ T[Tϱ ]}
with the corresponding shading Y Tϱ (ψTϱ (T )) = ψTϱ (Y (T )). This gives a sticky Kakeya configuration
(TTϱ , Y Tϱ ) of tubes on scale δ/ϱ, i.e. TTϱ is a collection of δ/ϱ-tubes that is almost AD-regular and
satisfies the Convex Wolff axiom.
   Here it is crucial that T is assumed to be almost AD-regular: if we start with an arbitrary set of
tubes satisfying Convex Wolff axioms, then TTϱ might fail to have the same property. Indeed, T could
be a well-spaced collection of tubes in the sense that every ϱ-tube contains at most one tube from
T (see [8]). So the rescaled collection TTϱ consists of a single tube which violates the convex Wolff
axiom.1 The stickiness assumption makes the problem invariant under both operations and it is used
crucially in the proof.
   The basic property of rescaling and thickening is the following multiplicity bound:
(2)                                          µ(T) ⪅ µ(Tϱ )µ(TTϱ ).

  1In the notation of [31], we work with the Convex Frostman Wolff axiom here. If we instead consider the Convex
Katz–Tao Wolff axiom, then in the well-spaced example TTϱ is also Katz–Tao, but the thickening Tϱ might fail to be
Katz–Tao.
6                                     HONG WANG, DMITRII ZAKHAROV

Indeed, fix p ∈ U (T, Y ). Assuming appropriate regularity properties, there are ≈ µ(Tϱ ) many ϱ-tubes
through p. For each such tube Tϱ , we can estimate the number of δ-tubes through p and which are
contained in Tϱ by µ(TTϱ ). Summing over Tϱ gives (2).
   Now by the maximality of κ, we can estimate µ(Tϱ ) ⪅ ϱ−κ and µ(TTϱ ) ⪅ (δ/ϱ)−κ and so applying
(2) matches the assumed lower bound µ(T) ⪆ δ −κ . So if T is a sticky Kakeya set with maximum
multiplicity, then so are Tϱ and TTϱ for every intermediate scale δ ≪ ϱ ≪ 1. This means that we can
replace T with the thickened or rescaled set of tubes whenever that makes the problem more structured.
   The first key observation (dating back to the work of Katz–Laba–Tao [12], see also Tao’s blogpost
[27]) is that several thickening and rescaling moves let us reduce to the case when the set E = U (T, Y )
has an extra ‘global grain’ structure. Namely, we claim that after a sequence of thickening and rescaling
moves one can write:
(3)                         E = {(x, y + f (z)x, z), z ∈ Z, y ∈ Yz , x ∈ Xy,z },
where:
     (i) Z ⊂ [−1, 1] is ≈ 1-dense set, Yz ⊂ [−1, 1] is (δ, 1 − κ, ≈ 1)-AD-regular for each z ∈ Z and Xy,z
         is ≈ 1-dense for each z ∈ Z, y ∈ Yz ,
    (ii) f : Z → [−1, 1] is an ≈ 1-Lipschitz function.
Furthermore, the set of tubes T is compatible with the structure of E in the following sense. For every
p = (x, y + f (z)x, z) ∈ E, let Tp = {T ∈ T : p ∈ Y (T )} be the set of tubes through p. Then:
                                                                                       S
   (iii) Tp is a (δ, κ, ≈ 1)-AD-regular set of tubes. Moreover, for every ϱ ∈ (δ, 1), | p′ ∈Bϱ (p)∩E Tp′ |ϱ ≈
         ϱ−κ . This means that up to resolution ϱ, sets Tp′ are roughly the same for p′ within the same
         ϱ-ball.
   (iv) Tp is contained in a O(δ)-neighborhood of the plane Πp = p + ⟨(1, f (z), 0), (0, ξp , 1)⟩ for some
         ξp ∈ [−1, 1]. The function p 7→ ξp is a ≈ 1-Lipschitz function on E.
  Let us briefly outline how one can obtain such description of E. Start with a sticky Kakeya config-
uration (T, Y ) of maximum multiplicity δ −κ . Let E = U (T, Y ).
  Let ϱ = δ 1/2 and consider a ϱ-ball Bϱ centered at one of the points p ∈ E. The number of ϱ-tubes
Tϱ intersecting with Bϱ is upper bounded by µ(Tϱ ) ≲ ϱ−κ . Let Tϱ,Bϱ denote this collection of ϱ-tubes.
On the other hand, for typical p′ ∈ Bϱ ∩ E we claim that
(4)                             #{Tϱ ∈ Tϱ,Bϱ : ∃T ∈ Tp′ , T ⊂ Tϱ } ⪆ ϱ−κ .
The left hand side counts the number of thick tubes through the ball Bϱ which contain a thin tube
passing through a fixed point p′ inside the ball. To see (4) we use double counting: there are δ −κ
many tubes through p′ and for each Tϱ there are at most µ(TTϱ ) ⪅ (δ/ϱ)−κ many tubes through p′
that are contained in Tϱ . Since the right hand side of (4) is roughly |Tϱ,Bϱ |, this gives a very strong
local structural property: for typical tube Tϱ ∈ Tϱ,Bϱ we have
(5)                     E ∩ Bϱ ≈ union of parallel δ × δ × ϱ tubes parallel to Tϱ .
Indeed, for a fixed Tϱ , a large fraction of points p′ ∈ E ∩ Bϱ has a tube T such that p′ ∈ T ⊂ Tϱ .
But then T ∩ Bϱ is a δ × δ × ϱ tube and the direction of this tube in within O(ϱ) of the direction of
Tϱ . Since we chose ϱ so that δ/ϱ = ρ/1, the direction vectors of these tubes are both defined up to
precision ∼ ϱ, giving (5).
   Pick a pair of tubes Tϱ , Tϱ′ ∈ Tϱ,Bϱ that make angle ≳ 1 with each other (if all tubes make angle
⩽ θ ≪ 1 then we can get a gain in (2) by decomposing at scale θ). Then applying (5) we see that
E ∩ Bϱ is roughly a union of parallel δ × ϱ × ϱ slabs in the direction spanned by the directions of Tϱ , Tϱ′ .
Any other tube Tϱ′′ ∈ Tϱ,Bϱ must be coplanar with Tϱ , Tϱ′ up to resolution ϱ, otherwise (5) implies
|Nδ̃ E| ≈ |E| for some δ̃ ≫ δ, which would contradict the maximality of κ.
   In particular, for every p′ , the set of tubes Tp′ passing through p′ must make angle at most ϱ = δ/ϱ
with the δ × ϱ × ϱ slab containing p′ . In particular, Tp′ is contained in a common ϱ × 1 × 1 slab centered
at p′ . By thickening T to scale ϱ and replacing (δ, ϱ) by (ϱ, ϱ1/2 ), we may assume that Tp′ is contained
                   KAKEYA AND FURSTENBERG PROBLEMS FOR STICKY SETS OF TUBES                                7

in a δ-neighborhood of a plane Πp′ passing through p′ (for all p′ ∈ E). On this new scale, there are
again δ × ϱ × ϱ local slabs coming from the argument in the previous paragraph. These slabs must be
aligned with the planes Πp′ that capture δ-tubes passing through each point p′ ∈ E ∩ Bϱ .
                                                                             (1)       (m)
   Now fix a ϱ-tube Tϱ ∈ Tϱ and cover E ∩ Tϱ by disjoint ϱ-balls Bϱ , . . . , Bϱ where m ≈ ϱ−1 .
                                                                (i)
The above argument applies inside each of the balls Bϱ . Consider the rescaled tube configuration
(T̃, Ỹ ) = (TTϱ , Y Tϱ ). After rotating coordinates, we can think of Tϱ as the vertical ϱ-tube around the
Z-axes, so that the affine rescaling map ψTϱ : Tϱ → B1 is given by
                                                                    
                                                                x y
                                           ψTϱ : (x, y, z) 7→    , ,z .
                                                                ϱ ϱ
                              (i)
Under this map, each ball Bϱ is mapped to a horizontal ϱ × 1 × 1 slab. The set of parallel δ × ϱ × ϱ
                     (i)
slabs covering E ∩ Bϱ is mapped to a set of parallel ϱ × 1 × ϱ tubes contained in the horizontal slab.
Note that for each slab, the common direction of these tubes could be different. In other words, we
can write the set Ẽ = U (T̃, Ỹ ) as
                     Ẽ ≈ Nϱ {(x, y + f (z)x, z), z ∈ ϱZ ∩ [0, 1], y ∈ Yz , x ∈ [0, 1]}.
Here each fixed value of z corresponds to a horizontal ϱ-slab, the vector (1, f (z), 0) is the direction
vector of parallel tubes in this slab, and y ∈ Yz parameterizes the offset of these tubes. So after
replacing T with T̃ this gives the structure declared in (3). By tracking through the construction one
can verify that it satisfies the properties (i)–(iv) stated above.

2.1.2. Analysis of E: first additive relation. We now study the set E given by (3). We will use the
interaction between the structure of E and the set of tubes T to deduce some useful additive relations
between the sets Yz , the directions sets of tubes Tp , the slope function f and the slopes ξp . After that
the problem can be analyzed using sum-product theorems. There are actually two different rather
reductions to a sum-product problem. In R3 , either one of these reduction suffices to prove the sticky
Kakeya conjecture. We use one of them in the sticky Furstenberg problem. For sticky Kakeya in R4 ,
we will need to use both.
   For a tube T ∈ T let θ(T ) ∈ [−1, 1]2 denote the vector such that the direction vector of T is
(θ(T ), 1). By property (iv), namely that the set of tubes Tp is contained in a common plane Πp =
p + ⟨(1, f (z), 0), (0, ξp , 1)⟩, we may write θ(Tp ) = {(φ, ξp + f (z)φ), φ ∈ Φp } where Φp ⊂ [−1, 1] is a
(δ, κ, ≈ 1)-AD-regular set. By (iii), if |p−p′ | ⩽ ϱ, then we have Nϱ Φp ≈ Nϱ Φp′ , i.e. the ϱ-neighborhoods
of these sets are essentially the same. Fix some intermediate scale ϱ = δ µ ∈ (δ, 1) (e.g. µ = 1/4 will
do), consider a ϱ-ball q = Bϱ (p0 ) centered at a fixed point p0 = (x0 , y0 + f (z0 )x0 , z0 ) ∈ E. We study
the set E(q) = E ∩ q. Let Φq ⊂ ϱZ ∩ [−1, 1] be the set so that Nϱ Φq ≈ Nϱ Φp for all p ∈ E(q).
Consider the sets Z(q) = Z ∩ Nϱ z0 and Y (q) = Nϱ3 Yz0 ∩ Nϱ2 y0 . (Later on we use Y (q) to denote
a slightly different set that is basically Yz0 ∩ Nϱ y0 ; the Y (q) defined here is a piece of that set that
is important in the end, so we skip this distinction in this discussion.) The set Z(q) is an ≈ 1 dense
subset in a ϱ-interval around z0 and Y (q) is a rescaled (ϱ, 1 − κ, ≈ 1)-AD-regular set contained in the
ϱ2 -neighborhood of y0 .
   The first additive relation is as follows:
(6)                 Y (q) ≈ Y (q) + (z − z ′ )(f (z) − f (z ′ ))(Φq − Φq ),   z, z ′ ∈ Z(q).
More precisely, an ≈ 1 fraction of tuples (y, z, z ′ , φ1 , φ2 ) ∈ Y (q) × Z(q)2 × Φ2q has the property that
y + (z − z ′ )(f (z) − f (z ′ ))(φ1 − φ2 ) ∈ Y (q).
  This relation is new and it is the key observation of this paper. The previous work on sticky Kakeya
— Tao’s outline [27] and the subsequent argument of Wang–Zahl [32] — make use of a related but
not equivalent local symmetry. In particular, the flexibility of choosing the angles φ1 , φ2 in (6) from a
κ-dimensional set Φq is a new feature of this and other relations appearing in this paper.
8                                           HONG WANG, DMITRII ZAKHAROV

   We would like to use (6) in order to prove that Y (q) ≈ Nϱ2 y0 (i.e. Y (q) is 1-dimensional after
appropriate rescaling). An important property of (6) is that it captures the difference between the
fields R and C. Namely, one could run the proof strategy that we outline here in an attempt to prove the
sticky Kakeya estimate over complex numbers (replace tubes by neighborhoods of complex lines in C3 ,
etc). The reduction so far was insensitive to the base field (except for the fact that we needed an access
to sufficiently many scales). However, at some point one needs to see a difference since a direct analogue
of Theorem 2.1 fails over R3 : take E to be a δ-neighborhood of a Hermitian quadric in C3 and T to be
a set of complex tubes contained in it. Then the set T will satisfy the Convex Wolff axiom with respect
to the class of ‘complex convex sets’, i.e. sets of the form U = {z1 v1 + z2 v2 + z3 v3 | zi ∈ C, |zi | ⩽ 1}
for v1 , v2 , v3 ∈ C3 .
   This example manifests as the following complex example satisfying (6). Take Y (q) = Φq = [−1, 1] ⊂
C, Z(q) = [−1, 1] + i[−1, 1] ⊂ C and set f (z) = z. Then (6) holds as:
                            (z − z ′ )(f (z) − f (z ′ ))(φ1 − φ2 ) = |z − z ′ |2 (φ1 − φ2 ) ∈ R.
As “dimC R = 1/2”, this gives a local example over C with κ = 1/2. In what follows we need to show
that this cannot happen over R. As is now customary, we need to use a sum-product theorem to do
so.
    We will give a justification to (6) later, first let us see how it can be used to conclude the proof. Let
z1 , z2 , z1′ , z2′ ∈ Z(q) be arbitrary and apply (6) with (z, z ′ ) ∈ {(z1 , z1′ ), (z1 , z2′ ), (z2 , z1′ ), (z2 , z2′ )}. Write
Q(z, z ′ ) = (z − z ′ )(f (z) − f (z ′ )). Using the following ‘polarization identity’
                       Q(z1 , z1′ ) − Q(z1 , z2′ ) − Q(z2 , z1′ ) + Q(z2 , z2′ )
                                   = −(z1 − z2 )(f (z1′ ) − f (z2′ )) − (z1′ − z2′ )(f (z1 ) − f (z2 )),
we obtain a polarized version of (6):
(7)           Y (q) ≈ Y (q) + ((z1 − z2 )(f (z1′ ) − f (z2′ )) + (z1′ − z2′ )(f (z1 ) − f (z2 )))(Φq − Φq ).
   Let V (z) = (z, f (z)) and V ′ (z ′ ) = (f (z ′ ), z ′ ). Similarly to the proof of the ABC sum-product theorem
[21], we view the z-expression as a scalar product of vectors V (z1 ) − V (z2 ) = (z1 − z2 , f (z1 ) − f (z2 ))
and V ′ (z1′ ) − V ′ (z2′ ) = (f (z1′ ) − f (z2′ ), z1′ − z2′ ). For a fixed pair (z1′ , z2′ ) we can then write the set
                      {(z1 − z2 )(f (z1′ ) − f (z2′ )) + (z1′ − z2′ )(f (z1 ) − f (z2 )), z1 , z2 ∈ Z(q)}
as
                         (Γ − Γ) · (V (z1′ ) − V (z2′ )),    Γ = {V (z) = (z, f (z)), z ∈ Z(q)}.
                                                                       f (z ′ )−f (z ′ )
The slope λ of the vector V (z1′ ) − V (z2′ ) is given by λ = z1′ −z ′ 2 . Thus the set of slopes λ taken over
                                                                           1   2
all pairs (z1′ , z2′ ) is the set of slopes of the graph Γ′ = {V ′ (z ′ ) = (z ′ , f (z ′ )), z ′ ∈ Z(q)} (which of course
is just a reflection of Γ but it is convenient to keep them separate). To control the set of slopes of Γ′ we
can apply the radial projections theorem of of Orponen–Shmerkin–Wang [20]: on a discretized level,
it states that if Γ′ is not contained in a tube then the set of slopes has ‘dimension’ min(dim Γ′ , 1) = 1.
As long as the function f (z) cannot be approximated by a linear function in z on the set Z(q), the
graph Γ′ is not concentrated in any tube and we can apply radial projections to Γ′ . Thus, the set of
               f (z ′ )−f (z ′ )
slopes λ = z11 −z2 2 is 1-dimensional (more precisely, it is a (ϱ, 1, ≈ 1)-set). Note that this step fails
over C if f (z) = z.
   By Kaufman’s projection theorem, πλ (Γ) is 1-dimensional for a typical λ taken from a 1-dimensional
set. Combining this information over all z1′ , z2′ and pruning down if needed, we conclude that the set
of expressions (z1 − z2 )(f (z1′ ) − f (z2′ )) + (z1′ − z2′ )(f (z1 ) − f (z2 )) is 1-dimensional. Thus, in light of (7),
the set Y (q) must be 1-dimensional as well. On the other hand, property (i) of E states that Y (q) is
(1 − κ)-AD-regular. So κ = 0 and we are done.
   It remains to consider the case when f (z) can be approximated by a linear function on the set Z(q).
This corresponds to the C 2 -smooth case in [32] and in that paper it was dealt with by applying a
                    KAKEYA AND FURSTENBERG PROBLEMS FOR STICKY SETS OF TUBES                                        9

non-linear restricted projections theorem. Here we take a different route and use (6) again. Indeed,
suppose that f (z) ≈ az + b holds for some a, b ∈ R on the set Z(q). Plugging this in, gives

(8)                           Y (q) ≈ Y (q) + a(z − z ′ )2 (Φq − Φq ), z, z ′ ∈ Z(q).

If a ⪆ 1 then a(z − z ′ )2 (φ1 − φ2 ) takes a 1-dimensional set of values and so we conclude that Y (q) is
1-dimensional, as desired. So we must have a ≪ 1. In particular, |f (z) − f (z ′ )| ≲ a|z − z ′ | ≪ |z − z ′ |.
This means that the slope function f has slower than Lipschitz variation on scale ϱ.
   We can apply this argument at every scale ϱ and conclude that |f (z) − f (z ′ )| ≲ |z − z ′ |1+ε on Z for
some ε > 0 (in fact one can get ε = 1). If this were the case for the whole interval [−1, 1] instead of Z,
then this would imply that f is a constant function. In this situation, one can check that E decomposes
into a union of parallel slabs and one quickly reaches a contradiction with the Convex Wolff axiom of T.
However, the set Z can and will be quite ‘porous’ and so f can have ‘Hölder exponent’ 1 + ε with ε > 0
and not be constant. Nevertheless, we can use a geometric argument to show that a sticky Kakeya
configuration with a slope function like this must always violate the Convex Wolff axiom. Roughly
speaking, we fix a tube T ∈ T and a point p on it. Look at all tubes T ′ that intersect T within distance
ϱ from p and then look at tubes T ′′ that intersect T ′ within distance ϱ and make angle O(ϱ) with T .
We show that all such tubes T ′′ are ‘trapped’ in a convex box of dimensions ϱ1+ε × ϱ × 1 around T .
By iterating this construction and applying a version of (6) we show that the number of tubes that
accumulate in this box exceeds the amount allowed by the Convex Wolff axiom. This contradiction
shows that κ = 0 and completes the proof of Theorem 2.1.

2.1.3. Second additive relation. We now describe a second additive relation that can be used to give
another proof of the sticky Kakeya conjecture in R3 . Recall from (iv) that ξp describes the slope of
the plane Πp through p which contains all tubes Tp . Fix some p0 = (x0 , y0 + f (z0 )x0 , z0 ) ∈ E, let
X(q) = Xy0 ,z0 ∩ Nϱ x0 , and define a function ξ : X(q) → [−1, 1] by ξ(x) = ξ(x,y0 +f (z0 )x,z0 ) . Note that
ξ(x) is a ≈ 1-Lipschitz function of x (since ξp is Lipschitz in p by (iv)). Consider the graphs
                                                                             
                            x                                       z
                  A=              , x ∈ X(q)      and B =                 , z ∈ Z(q) .
                           ξ(x)                                  −f (z)

Then our second key relation states that

(9)                                       A ≈ A + (B − B)(Φq − Φq ).

More explicitly, we have

(10)                     ξ(x + (z − z ′ )(φ1 − φ2 )) ≈ ξ(x) − (f (z) − f (z ′ ))(φ1 − φ2 )

for an ≈ 1-dense set of tuples (x, z, z ′ , φ1 , φ2 ) ∈ X(q)×Z(q)2 ×Φ2q . The representation (9) is similar to
the ABC-sum-product theorem of Orponen–Shmerkin [21] which roughly states that if A, B, C ⊂ [−1, 1]
are sets such that A ≈ A + BC then dim A ⩾ min(1, dim B + dim C). Here we have a two-dimensional
version of their theorem: A, B ⊂ R2 and C = Φq ⊂ [−1, 1]. Since A, B are graphs, we only need
the special case when dim A = dim B = 1. If B is not concentrated in any tube, then by the radial
                                                                                                b −b′
projections theorem, the set of slopes Λ ⊂ [−1, 1] of B is 1-dimensional. For each slope λ = b12 −b′2 , where
                                                                                                            1
                                                                                      |A|
b = (b1 , b2 ), b′ = (b′1 , b′2 ) ∈ B, the identity (9) then implies that |πλ (A)| ≲ |Φ q|
                                                                                           , where πλ (x, y) = x + λy.
Indeed, each fiber of A above the projection πλ contains a dilate of the set Φq − Φq . So the set A
drops in size along a ≈ 1 dimensional set of orthogonal projections. Kaufman projection theorem then
implies that dim A ⩾ 1 + κ. So κ = 0, as desired. The case when B is contained in a tube corresponds
to f being approximated by a linear function. This case can be dealt with using (6) as previously.
Thus, (9) gives an alternative way to rule out the ‘non-linear’ case.
10                                     HONG WANG, DMITRII ZAKHAROV

2.1.4. Deducing the additive relations. We now explain how to arrive at (6) and (9). Let Yz (q) =
Nϱ3 Yz ∩ Nϱ2 y0 . The first step is to show that Yz (q) ≈ Yz ′ (q) for any z, z ′ ∈ Z(q).
    After a change of coordinates, we may assume that p0 = 0, 0 ∈ Φq and that ξp0 = 0. So by (iv), for
typical p ∈ E ∩ Bϱ2 (p0 ) there is a ‘vertical’ tube T ∈ Tp through p, i.e. |θ(T )| ≲ ϱ2 . Thus, for typical
p1 = (x1 , y1 + f (z0 )x1 , z0 ) with |p1 − p0 | ≲ ϱ2 we have p + (z − z0 )(θ(T ), 1) ∈ Nδ E for many z ∈ Z. By
taking z ∈ Z(q) (i.e. |z − z0 | ≲ ϱ) and using |θ(T )| ≲ ϱ2 , we get that y1 + O(ϱ3 ) ∈ Yz (q).
    So the sets Yz (q) are approximately the same for different z ∈ Z(q). Thus, in order to prove (6),
it suffices to take z ′ = z0 . For convenience we use z0 = 0 and f (z0 ) = 0. Consider a triple of points
p1 , p2 , p ∈ E(q) forming a ‘V -shape’: pi = (xi , yi , 0) ∈ E ∩ {z = 0} lie on level 0, p = (x, y + f (z)x, z)
lies on level z and there is a pair of tubes T1 , T2 ∈ T connecting them, i.e. p1 , p ∈ T1 and p2 , p ∈ T2 .
So we have
                                            p = p1 + z(θ(T1 ), 1) + O(δ)
                                         = p2 + z(θ(T2 ), 1) + O(δ).
Write θ(Ti ) = (φi , ξp + f (z)φi ) + O(δ), with ξp as in property (iv) and φi ∈ Φp for the set Φp
parameterizing the slopes of tubes Tp inside the plane Πp . Plugging in these into the above relation
between p, p1 , p2 gives
                         (x, y + f (z)x) = (x1 , y1 ) + z(φ1 , ξp + f (z)φ1 ) + O(δ)
                                           = (x2 , y2 ) + z(φ2 , ξp + f (z)φ2 ) + O(δ).
Rearranging then yields:
                                      x2 − x1 = z(φ1 − φ2 ) + O(δ),
                                      y2 − y1 = zf (z)(φ1 − φ2 ) + O(δ)
   By (iii) the set of slopes Φp is essentially independent from the choice of p ∈ q = Bϱ (0) up to
precision ϱ. That is, for some set Φq ⊂ ϱZ ∩ [−1, 1] we have Nϱ Φp ≈ Nϱ Φq . Thus, we can write
φi = φi + O(ϱ) for some φi ∈ Φq . In this computation we can choose y1 ∈ Y (q), z ∈ Z(q) and φi ∈ Φq
essentially arbitrarily and so (6) follows (recall that we chose coordinates so that z ′ = 0).
   To obtain (9) or (10), we additionally write
                               θ(Ti ) = (φi , ξpi + f (z0 )φi ) = (φi , ξp + f (z)φi )
This implies that
                               ξp2 − ξp1 = (f (z) − f (z0 ))(φ2 − φ1 ) + O(δ).
Since p2 = p1 + z(φ2 − φ1 , 0, 0) + O(ϱ2 ), z0 = 0, and ξp is Lipschitz in p, this gives the desired relation
on ξ(x2 ) and ξ(x1 ).
   Variants of the computations outlined in this part will appear many times throughout our arguments.
We now turn to describe the additional ideas required for the sticky Furstenberg and higher dimensional
sticky Kakeya.
2.2. Sticky Furstenberg. In the Furstenberg setting, the set of tubes T is 2t-AD-regular for some
t ∈ (0, 2] and the shading Y (T ) is assumed to be (δ, s)-Frostman. For simplicity, we will assume
that Y (T ) is in fact (δ, s)-AD-regular, so that Y (T ) ∩ Bϱ (p) is a rescaled (δ/ϱ, s)-Frostman for every
p ∈ Y (T ) and ϱ ∈ (δ, 1). This assumption can be removed by carefully choosing the intermediate scale
ϱ.
   After a sequence of rescaling and thickening moves similarly to the initial reduction above, we can
parameterize the set E as follows
                                                  [
(11)                                         E=       Az × {z}
                                                     z∈Z

where Z ⊂ [−1, 1] is a (δ, s, ≈ 1)-set and Az ⊂ [−1, 1]2 is a (δ, 2t − κ, ≈ 1)-AD-regular set for every
z ∈ Z. Furthermore, for every tube T ∈ T we have Y (T ) ≈ E ∩ T , i.e. Y (T ) is essentially the same as
Z after coordinate projection. The numerology for the dimension of Az comes from a double counting
                   KAKEYA AND FURSTENBERG PROBLEMS FOR STICKY SETS OF TUBES                               11


identity: |E| ≈ |Z||Az | ≈ |T||Y (T )|
                              |Tp | . This is similar to the rescaling step in the sticky Kakeya argument,
except that slices Az do not necessarily have a global grains structure.
   Recall that one of the early steps in the sticky Kakeya proof was to reduce to the case when the
set of tubes Tp through a point p ∈ E is coplanar. For the Furstenberg problem, this step is more
complicated and we need to consider more general sets Tp . First, by the rescaling symmetry, we may
assume that Tp is (δ, κ, ≈ 1)-AD-regular, for some κ ⩾ 0, and we choose κ to be the largest possible
exponent. Our goal is to prove an upper bound κ ⩽ κ0 (s, t) = max(0, t − s, 2t − 2).
   In Section 5 we prove a useful structural result for almost AD-regular subsets in [−1, 1]d . By applying
this result to the set of directions θ(Tp ) ⊂ [−1, 1]2 , we can rescale and thicken the set of tubes and
obtain the following structure. For every p ∈ E the set of directions Θp = θ(Tp ) can be written as
                                 Θp = {(φ, ξ + αp φ), ξ ∈ Ξp , φ ∈ Φp,ξ },
where αp ∈ [−1, 1] is a slope depending on p, and for some parameter γ ∈ [0, κ], we have that
Ξp ⊂ [−1, 1] is a (δ, κ − γ, ≈ 1)-AD-regular set and Φp,ξ ⊂ [−1, 1] is a (δ, γ, ≈ 1)-AD-regular set.
Furthermore, we have that |Θp ∩ Tϱ | ≲ ϱκ−γ |Θp | for any (planar) ϱ-tube T ⊂ [−1, 1]2 and any ϱ ∈ [δ, 1]
(and this inequality is almost achieved by tubes at slope αp that cover Θp ). In other words, the set
Θp is contained in a union of parallel tubes of maximum density and all underlying sets are almost
AD-regular. The parameter γ ∈ [0, κ] quantifies how coplanar the tubes in Tp are and we need to
consider several cases depending on the value of γ.
   When γ is small, namely, if γ ⩽ s + κ − 1, then we obtain the desired estimate using the high-low
method. By considering the intersection E ∩ Bϱ (p) and rescaling, we obtain an orthogonal projections
problem on scale ϱ, where the the set of projections is given by the set Nϱ Θp (as before, this set does not
depend on the particular choice of a point in the ϱ-ball). The condition that Θ does not intersect with
tubes too much can be used to effectively estimate the overlap on the Fourier side and upper bound
the ‘high’ part. On the other hand, if ‘low’ dominates then E ∩ Bϱ (p) is locally a (2 + s)-dimensional
set. Comparing with (11) and recalling that Az is (2t − κ)-AD-regular, this leads to the desired bound
on κ. We refer to Section 9 for a short self-contained argument. The upper bound γ ⩽ s + κ − 1 is
precisely the right condition to make this estimate work.
   Next, we consider the intermediate case γ ∈ [s + κ − 1, 1 − s]. To simplify the notation a bit, let us
assume that for some fixed p ∈ E we have Θp = Φp × Ξp (i.e. αp = 0 and all sets Φp,ξ are the same)
where Φp , Ξp ⊂ [−1, 1] are γ- and (κ − γ)- almost-AD-regular
                                                           S     sets, respectively. Let q = Bϱ (p), we can
localize the representation (11) to q and write E ∩ q = z∈Z(q) Az (q) × {z}. Since the sets Nϱ Θp′ are
approximately the same for all p′ ∈ E ∩ q, this gives
                               Az ′ (q) ≈ Az (q) + (z ′ − z)(φ, ξ, 1) + O(ϱ2 )
for many z, z ′ ∈ Z(q) and φ ∈ Φp , ξ ∈ Ξp . Indeed, for each point in the z-slice, choose a tube
through it pointing roughly in the (φ, ξ, 1) direction and move along this tube to level z ′ . By changing
coordinates we may assume that 0 ∈ Φp ∩ Ξp , meaning that all sets Az (q) are approximately the same
up to resolution ϱ2 . Assume for a moment that the set Az (q) is also a cartesian product X(q) × Y (q)
for some sets X(q) and Y (q). Then the above additive relation can be split into x- and y-coordinates:
                                    X(q) ≈ X(q) + (Z(q) − Z(q))Φp
                                    Y (q) ≈ Y (q) + (Z(q) − Z(q))Ξp .
We can now apply the ABC-sum-product theorem of Orponen and Shmerkin [21] to conclude that
              dim X(q) ⩾ min(1, dim Z(q) + dim Φp ) = min(1, s + γ) = s + γ,
               dim Y (q) ⩾ min(1, dim Z(q) + dim Ξp ) = min(1, s + (κ − γ)) = s + κ − γ,
where we used the assumption that γ is in the intermediate range [s + κ − 1, 1 − s] to compute the
minimums. By combining these estimates we conclude that dim Az (q) ⩾ 2s + κ. On the other hand,
we know that Az is (2t − κ)-AD-regular and so we get κ ⩽ t − s ⩽ κ0 (s, t), as desired. In this argument
12                                    HONG WANG, DMITRII ZAKHAROV

we used some cartesian product structure assumptions on the set of directions Θp and the sets Az (q),
these assumptions are not essential and can be removed.
   One caveat in this part of the argument is that we need to assume that both sets Φp and Ξp have
positive dimension. Indeed, if either of the sets consists of the single element 0, then the additive
relation on X(q) or Y (q), respectively, does not give any new information. In case of Φp this is not
really a problem: heuristically, by aligning the coordinate system we may arrange Φp to have at least
two well-separated points, and so even if Φp is ‘zero-dimensional’, we can still apply (trivial case of)
ABC-sum-product. On the other hand, the case of Ξp = {0} requires extra care. We will comment on
this case below.
   It remains to consider the ‘almost coplanar’ case γ ∈ [1 − s, 1]. We can apply the same ABC-sum-
product argument as above to the set X(q) to conclude that dim X(q) ⩾ min(1, s + γ) = 1. In other
words, the set Az (q) fully fills out horizontal lines (with the vertical offsets given by Y (q)). This means
that the set E ∩ q has global grains, just as in the sticky Kakeya setting. By rescaling and thickening
we can then reduce to the case when we can write
                             E = {(x, y + f (z)x, z), z ∈ Z, y ∈ Yz , x ∈ Xy,z },
where Z ⊂ [−1, 1] is an s-dimensional set, Yz ⊂ [−1, 1] is (2t − κ − 1)-AD-regular and Xy,z ⊂ [−1, 1]
is ≈ 1-dense. Furthermore, we have appropriate versions of properties (i), (ii), (iii), (iv) from Section
2.1. The same identity (6) then holds in this setting:
                     Y (q) ≈ Y (q) + (z − z ′ )(f (z) − f (z ′ ))(φ1 − φ2 ),   z, z ′ ∈ Z(q).
Here the angles φ1 , φ2 range over a γ-dimensional set Φq (if we had cartesian products Θp = Φp × Ξp
then we can take Φq ≈ Nϱ Φp ). If the slope function f cannot be approximated by a linear function on
Z(q) then the polarization argument and the applications of radial projection / Kaufman projection
theorems imply that
                                      Y (q) ≈ Y (q) + S(q)(Φq − Φq ),
where S(q) is an at least s-dimensional set produced from Z(q) and f (the set of scalar products of
difference sets of two graphs Γ and Γ′ ). So by the ABC-sum-product theorem we obtain
                      dim Y (q) ⩾ min(1, dim S(q) + dim Φq ) ⩾ min(1, s + γ) ⩾ 1,
giving κ ⩽ 2t − 2 ⩽ κ0 (s, t), as desired. In the case when f is a locally approximated by a linear
function with non-zero derivative, a direct application of (6) again leads to a desired bound. In the
case when f has ‘Hölder exponent’ 1 + ε, we reach a contradiction with the Convex Wolff axiom, very
similarly to the sticky Kakeya case in R3 .
   A similar argument applies to the aforementioned edge case Ξ = {0}. In this case we have γ = κ,
the ABC sum-product argument in the X-direction gives dim X(q) ⩾ min(1, s+κ) and we need to deal
with the case when the minimum is achieved on the second term. Here we can use a slightly simpler
additive relation on Y (q):
(12)                         Y (q) ≈ Y (q) + f (z)(x2 − x1 ) − z(ξ(x2 ) − ξ(x1 ))
where z ∈ Z(q), x1 , x2 ∈ X(q) and ξ is the slope function defined in the second additive relation.
We view the additive term as the scalar product between vectors (f (z), z) and (x, −ξ(x)). Using the
Furstenberg set estimate we show that the set of such dot products has dimension at least
                                    dim X(q) + dim Z(q)   (s + κ) + s
                                                        ⩾             .
                                             2                 2
On the other hand, dim Y (q) ⩽ (2t − κ) − dim X(q), which leads to the bound κ ⩽ 45 (t − s) ⩽ t − s,
as desired. The relation (12) is essentially the one used in the previous sticky Kakeya argument [32],
[27].
                   KAKEYA AND FURSTENBERG PROBLEMS FOR STICKY SETS OF TUBES                              13

2.3. Four dimensional Sticky Kakeya. We state the problem in arbitrary dimension d since the
initial reduction and some of the arguments work for all dimensions, later we specialize to d = 4.
   Let T be a (δ, d − 1, ≈ 1)-AD-regular set of tubes in Rd and let Y (T ) be a ≈ 1-dense shading on
T. Suppose that T satisfies the Convex Wolff axiom. We start by applying rescaling and thickening to
reduce to the case when E = U (T, Y ) has some special form.             S
   We may assume that the set of tubes Tp through a point p ∈ E = T Y (T ) is a (δ, κ, ≈ 1)-AD-
regular set, where κ > 0 is the largest possible exponent. By the multilinear Kakeya argument, we
may assume that Tp is essentially contained in a common hyperplane. In dimension d ⩾ 4 it might
happen that Tp is contained in a smaller dimensional subspace. Thus, we need to split into several
cases depending on the dimension of the span of Tp . So there exists some ℓ ∈ {2, . . . , d − 1} such that
                                                        (ℓ)
Tp is contained in a O(δ) neighborhood of an ℓ-plane Πp and does not concentrate too much in any
ℓ − 1-plane. By a multilinear Kakeya argument using (5), E locally fills out

                                                                   . . × δ 1/2}
                                             . . × δ} × |δ 1/2 × .{z
                                      δ| × .{z
                                           d−ℓ                     ℓ

parallel convex sets (previously we had this with ℓ = 2). Rescaling turns these into (ℓ − 1)-dimensional
horizontal global grains:
(13)                        E = {(x, y + f (z)x, z), z ∈ Z, y ∈ Yz , x ∈ Xy,z }
where now Z ⊂ [−1, 1] is ≈ 1-dense, Yz ⊂ [−1, 1]d−ℓ is (d − ℓ − κ)-AD-regular and Xy,z ⊂ [−1, 1]ℓ−1 is
≈ 1-dense. The ‘slope function’ f (z) is now a (d − ℓ) × (ℓ − 1)-matrix. There are two special cases that
generalize fairly easily from the 3-dimensional sticky Kakeya argument in Section 2.1. The first case is
when the grains have codimension 1, i.e. ℓ = d − 1. Then the set Y (q) ⊂ [−1, 1] is (1 − κ)-dimensional
and f (z) is a linear map from Rℓ−1 to R. A direct analogue of (6) gives
                            Y (q) ≈ Y (q) + (z ′ − z)(f (z ′ ) − f (z))(Φq − Φq ).
The important difference is that now Φq ⊂ [−1, 1]ℓ−1 is a κ-AD-regular set which does not concentrate
in any slab. Thus, for ℓ = d − 1, we get dot products between the vector f (z ′ ) − f (z) ∈ Rℓ−1 and
Φq . If the function f (z) is not approximated by a linear function on Z(q), then we can find a pair
φ1 , φ2 ∈ Φq such that the function fφ1 ,φ2 : z 7→ f (z) · (φ1 − φ2 ) is also not approximated by a linear
function (using that Φq spans Rℓ in a robust way). We can now use fφ1 ,φ2 to proceed exactly as in
the outline for the sticky Kakeya and conclude that Y (q) is locally 1-dimensional. The cases when f
is locally linear or constant can be dealt with in a similar manner.
   Let us point out a major difficulty in trying to extend this argument to the case when ℓ ⩽ d − 2.
The identity (6) is still true but it becomes more difficult to use. Indeed, the set Y (q) on the left hand
side now d − ℓ − κ dimensional. On the other hand, z ∈ Z ranges over a 1-dimensional set and Φq is
κ-dimensional set. Thus, in order to obtain a contradiction, we need to show that a 1-dimensional set
and a κ-dimensional set together produce a ⩾ d − ℓ − κ ⩾ 2 − κ dimensional set. It is plausible that
something like this should be true given a sufficiently strong non-linearity assumption on f and some
non-degeneracy condition of Φq . However, proving this seems difficult.
   Another nice special case is when ℓ = 2. (One can see that when d = 4, ℓ = 2 and ℓ = d − 1
are exactly the two cases we need to consider.) Then we have Φp ⊂ [−1, 1] and for each p ∈ E and
we can write Θp = {(φ, ξp + f (z)φ), φ ∈ Φp } for some ξp ∈ [−1, 1]d−2 . By defining the function
ξ(x) = ξ(x,y0 +f (z0 )x,z0 ) for some fixed y0 , z0 , we have a direct analogue of (9):
(14)                                   A ≈ A + (B − B)(Φq − Φq )
where                                                                                
                            x                                               z
                  A=              , x ∈ X(q)          and     B=                  , z ∈ Z(q) .
                           ξ(x)                                           −f (z)
14                                        HONG WANG, DMITRII ZAKHAROV

Note that as before dim A = dim B = 1. By taking a generic linear projection Rd−1 → R2 we may
reduce to the case when A, B ⊂ [−1, 1]2 are 1-dimensional sets satisfying (14). This is exactly the
same setup as in the case of the sticky Kakeya in R3 and so the same argument applies.
   From this argument it follows that B must be concentrated in a tube, i.e. the function f is essentially
a locally linear function (one can verify that this property is preserved under generic projection to R2 ).
By using a version of the identity (8), we can use this to conclude that
(15)                                         Y (q) ≈ Y (q) + v · [−ϱ2 , ϱ2 ]
for some non-zero vector v ∈ Rd−2 (which depends on q). By rescaling into a ϱ2 -tube, this implies
that the set E fills out out 2-dimensional horizontal global grains. This is not a contradiction but it
does give us extra information. It turns out that this is a tricky case that requires additional ideas
and structures, which we discuss in the next paragraph.
   Let us now specialize to d = 4 and summarize what we have shown so far. First, we proved that
if Tp is 3-linear (i.e. robustly spans a 3-plane), then dim E = 4 and κ = 0. Then, assuming that Tp
is contained in a 2-plane, we showed that E must have 2-dimensional global grains (as opposed to
the 1-dimensional ones which we get ‘for free’). Let f˜(z) = (f˜1 (z), f˜2 (z)) denote a new slope function
parameterizing the 2-grains, then we can write
(16)                         E = {(x̃, ỹ + f˜(z)x̃, z), z ∈ Z, ỹ ∈ Ỹz , x̃ ∈ X̃ỹ,z },
where Ỹz ⊂ [−1, 1] is (1 − κ)-AD-regular and X̃ỹ,z ⊂ [−1, 1]2 is ≈ 1-dense. This is similar to the
representation (3) with ℓ = 3 except that the grains are no longer spanned by the directions of tubes
in Tp . The 1-grains parameterized by f and the 2-grains parameterized by f˜ are ‘compatible’ with
each other. For example for θ = θ(T ) ∈ Θp we can write
                             θ =(φ, ξp + f (z)φ) = (φ, ξ1 + f1 (z)φ, ξ2 + f2 (z)φ)
                               =(φ, ξp + f1 (z)φ, ξ˜p + f˜(z) · (φ, ξp + f1 (z)φ)).
By comparing the last coordinates we get the following relation:
                                             f2 (z) = f˜1 (z) + f˜2 (z)f1 (z).
We have another           which follows from the ℓ = 2 argument above. Recall that we showed that

   We use this information to analyze the set E ∩ q. By tracking down how tubes interact with the
structure of E, we obtain a somewhat more involved version of the key identity (6) on Y (q) which is
as follows:
(17)            Ỹ (q) ≈ Ỹ (q) + (z − z ′ )(f2 (z) − f2 (z ′ ) − f˜2 (z ′ )(f1 (z) − f1 (z ′ )))(Φq − Φq ),

(18)                           Y (q) ≈ Y (q) + (z − z ′ )(f (z) − f (z ′ ))(φ1 − φ2 ),
on the other hand, comparing the representations (13) and (16) we can write
                     (x, y1 + f1 (z ′ )x, y2 + f2 (z ′ )) = (x̃1 , x̃2 , ỹ + f˜1 (z ′ )x̃1 + f˜2 (z ′ )x̃2 )
                    KAKEYA AND FURSTENBERG PROBLEMS FOR STICKY SETS OF TUBES                                   15

and putting x = x̃1 = 0 gives representation
                                      Yz ′ = {(x̃2 , ỹ + f˜2 (z ′ )x̃2 ), ỹ ∈ Ỹz ′ },
where x̃2 ranges over a dense subset in [−1, 1]. Ignoring the slight difference between Yz ′ and Y (q), we
can apply an orthogonal projection along the vector (1, f˜2 (z ′ )) (i.e. take dot product with (−f˜2 (z ′ ), 1))
to both sides of (18) and arrive at (17).
   Now our task is to use (17) to show that Y (q) is locally 1-dimensional, or to arrive at a contradiction
with the Convex Wolff axiom. The analysis follows roughly the same route as before: we use a
polarization identity and then use radial and Kaufman projection theorems. Computations become
more complicated and we need to use the ‘C 1 -smoothness’ assumption on f .
   Assuming the normalization f ′ (z0 ) = (1, 0), the polarization step ultimately shows that
                        Y (q) ≈ Y (q) + (V (z1 ) − V (z2 )) · (U (z1′ ) − U (z2′ ))(Φq − Φq )
where V (z), U (z ′ ) ∈ R3 are 3-dimensional vectors given by
                                                           f2 (z ′ ) − 2z ′ f˜2 (z ′ )
                                                                                   
                                        z
                            V (z) =  z 2  , U (z ′ ) =           f˜2 (z ′ )        .
                                      f2 (z)                           z ′

Thus, to finish the proof, it suffices to show that the set of dot products V (z) · U (z ′ ) spans a 1-
dimensional subset in [−1, 1]. Note that {V (z1 ) − V (z2 ), z1 , z2 ∈ Z(q)} is at least 2-dimensional
set (consider the first two coordinates) and unless f2 is close to a quadratic polynomial, this set is
not concentrated in any slab. So a 3-dimensional radial projections theorem implies that the set
{V (z1 ) − V (z2 )} defines a 2-dimensional set of slopes. Since {U (z)} is a 1-dimensional set (consider
the last coordinate), a 3D Kaufman projection theorem implies that the dot product of {U (z)} with a
typical slope of {V (z1 ) − V (z2 )} is 1-dimensional. This shows that Y (q) is locally 1-dimensional and
so κ = 0, as desired. The case when f2 is locally a quadratic function can be dealt in an analogous
way to how we dealt with linear cases.

                                                     3. Notation
   In this section, we recall some definitions and lemmas from [21].
   The letter C will usually denote a constant, while the letter K will usually denote a parameter
depending on δ (e.g. K = Cδ −ϵ ).
   We say A ≲ B if A ⩽ CB for some constant C, and define A ≳ B, A ∼ B similarly. We write
A ≲n B to emphasize the implicit constant may depend on n. We also use A ≳δO(η) B to denote that
A ⩾ C1 δ C2 η B for some constant C1 , C2 > 0 and A ≈δO(η) B to denote C1−1 δ C2 η B ⩽ A ⩽ C1 δ −C2 η B.
   For a finite set A, |A| denotes cardinality of A. For a measurable and infinite set A (usually a ball
or a rectangle), |A| denotes the Lebesgue measure of A.
Definition 3.1. We say that a subset A of Rd is (δ, t, C)-set if the following holds: for every r ⩾ δ
and r-ball Br we have
                                         |A ∩ Br | ⩽ Crt |A|.
Additionally, we say that A is rescaled (δ, s, C)-set if there is a homothety ϕ : Rd → Rd such that
ϕ(A) ⊂ B1 and ϕ(A) is (δ, s, C)-set.
Definition 3.2. We say that a subset A of Rd is (δ, t, C)-Katz-Tao if the following holds: for every
r ⩾ δ and r-ball Br we have
                                     |A ∩ Br |δ ⩽ C(r/δ)t .
Definition 3.3. We say that a subset A of Rd is ([δ, ϱ], t, C)-AD-regular if the following holds: for
any a ∈ A and r ∈ [δ, ϱ] we have
                                      |A ∩ Br (a)|δ ∈ [C −1 (r/δ)t , C(r/δ)t ].
16                                   HONG WANG, DMITRII ZAKHAROV

   Typically, we will have ϱ = 1 in which case we say that A is (δ, t, C)-AD-regular.
   For any compact set A ⊂ Rd and r > 0, let Nr A := {x ∈ Rd : d(x, A) ⩽ r} denote the Euclidean
r-neighborhood of A.
Definition 3.4 (Dyadic cubes). If n ∈ Z and δ = 2−n , denote by Dδ (Rd ) := {[aδ, (a + 1)δ) × [bδ, (b +
1)δ) : a, b ∈ Z} the family of dyadic cubes in Rd of side-length δ. If P ⊂ Rd , denote
                                  Dδ (P) := {Q ∈ Dδ (Rd ) : Q ∩ P ̸= ∅}.
We abbreviate Dδ := Dδ ([0, 1)d ) for n ⩾ 0. If δ < ∆ ∈ 2−N and p ∈ Dδ , let p∆ denote the dyadic cube
p ∈ D∆ containing p.
   For a compact set A ⊂ Rd and r = 2−n we denote A(r) = a∈A a(r) = Dr (A) the union of dyadic
                                                             S            S

r-cubes covering A. We typically omit the brackets and write Ar = A(r) for the dyadic neighborhood
of A (apart from cases in which it might lead to confusion).
Definition 3.5. For a bounded set P ⊂ Rd and δ = 2−n , n ⩾ 0, define the dyadic δ-covering number
                                             |P |δ := |Dδ (P )|.
   The number |P |δ is comparable (up to a universal constant factor) to the least number of δ-balls
needed to cover P . (This latter definition is the more common definition of covering number.) If
P ⊂ Dδ is a set of dyadic cubes, we say that P is a (δ, s, C)-set if the union of δ-cubes in P is a
(δ, s, C)-set in the sense of Definition 3.1 and we use |P| to denote the number of dyadic cubes in P
and |P|∆ := |D∆ (P )| (hence |P|δ = |P|).
   For different parts of the proof it will be sometimes convenient to use slightly different definitions
of a tube. In most places, we define for (a, b) ∈ [−1, 1]d−1 × Rd−1 a δ-tube T as
(19)                          T = {(at + b + x, t), t ∈ [−1, 1], x ∈ Bδ (0)}.
Note that such T makes angle ≲ 1 with the vertical axes. For the purposes of rescaling and thickening
we use the following notion of dyadic δ-tubes.
Definition 3.6 (Dyadic δ-tubes). Let δ ∈ 2−N . A dyadic δ-tube is a set of the form T = ∪x∈p D(x),
where p ∈ Dδ ([−1, 1]d−1 × Rd−1 ), and D is the point-line duality map
                            D(a, b) := {(y, x) ∈ Rd−1 × R : y = ax + b} ⊂ Rd
sending the point (a, b) ∈ R2(d−1) to a corresponding line in Rd . Abusing the notation, we abbreviate
D(p) := ∪x∈p D(x). The collection of all dyadic δ-tubes is denoted
                                Tδ := {D(p) : p ∈ Dδ ([−1, 1]d−1 × Rd−1 )}.
A finite collection of dyadic δ-tubes {D(p)}p∈P is called a (δ, s, C)-set if P is a (δ, s, C)-set.
  If δ < ∆ ∈ 2−N and T ∈ Tδ , let T ∆ denote the unique dyadic tube T ∈ T∆ containing T .
   Note that for (a, b) ∈ [−1, 1]2(d−1) the dyadic δ-tube D(p) ∩ [−1, 1]d , where p = (a, b)δ ∈ Dδ , defines
an approximately the same set as the δ-tube T defined in (19). We can always switch between normal
and dyadic tubes and only lose constant factors.
   One drawback of dyadic tubes is that for T ∈ Tδ it might not be the case that the Euclidean
neighborhood Nδ T is contained in a ‘thicker’ tube T ϱ for some ϱ > δ. This happens if T is very close
to the boundary of the bigger dyadic tube. The following proposition is aimed at mitigating this issue
by randomly translating the set of dyadic tubes and thickening them slightly.
Proposition 3.1. Let T ⊂ Tδ and suppose that each T ∈ T is of the form D(p) for some p ∈
Dδ ([−1, 1]2(d−1) ). Let S ⩾ 2 and suppose that δ ∈ 2SZ and S ⩾ C log log(1/δ). Then there exists
        S

       • for every ϱ ∈ [2S δ, 2−S ] ∩ 2Z and T ∈ T′ϱ we have
                                                            S

hard to see that then we have
                                   |Tϱ (T2S ϱ )| ⩾ (1 − Cd 2−S )|Tϱ [T2S ϱ ]|,
i.e. most of the ϱ-tubes contained in T2S ϱ are ϱ-separated from the boundary.
    Let v ∈ [−1, 1]d be a uniformly random vector. One can check that for any fixed T ∈ T, with
                                                      S                                           S
probability at least 1 − C2−S , there exists T̃ ∈ T2 δ such that T + v ⊂ T̃ . Define T̃(v) ⊂ T2 δ to be

    S
T̃ 2 ϱ . Since S ⩾ C log log(1/δ), this probability is at least 1/2. So using linearity of expectation, we
can fix a value of v and define T′ to be the set of T̃ ∈ T̃(v) such that the property above holds.       □
  For a set of dyadic δ-tubes T and δ < ∆ ∈ 2−N , define the dyadic ∆-covering number |T|∆ := |D∆ (T)|
where D∆ (T) := {T ∈ T∆ : ∃T ∈ T, T = T ∆ }. When δ = ∆, we clearly have |T|δ = |T|.
Definition 3.7 (Slope set). The slope of a line ℓ = D(a, b) is defined to be the vector a ∈ Rd−1 , write
dir(ℓ) := a.
   If T = D(p) is a dyadic δ-tube, we define the slope set dir(T ) = ∪x∈p dir(D(x)). Thus dir(T ) is a
cube of sidelength δ. If T is a collection of dyadic δ-tubes, we write dir(T) := {dir(T ) : T ∈ T}.
   Moreover, writing p = (a, b) + [0, δ)2(d−1) , we denote θ(T ) = a. In other words, θ(T ) is the unique
element in the intersection dir(T ) ∩ δZd−1 .
Definition 3.8. Let T be a set of δ-tubes. We say that Y is a (δ, s, C)-dense shading on T if for any
T ∈ T, Y (T ) is a union of dyadic δ-cubes intersecting T and for any ϱ ∈ [δ, 1] and any Bϱ ,
                                       |Y (T ) ∩ Bϱ |δ ⩽ Cϱs |Y (T )|δ .

Definition 3.9 ((t, K)-AD regular set of tubes.). Let T be a set of dyadic δ-tubes. We say that T is
(t, K)-AD-regular if for any ϱ ∈ [δ, 1] ∩ 2−N and any T ∈ T,

(t, K)-AD-regular if (20) holds with T ϱ = Nϱ T . In applications we usually take K = δ −η for some
very small parameter η > 0.
   Let p be a ϱ-cube of the form (a, b) + [0, ϱ)2(d−1) . For a ϱ-tube T = D(p), we can define the

  We consider the natural choice t′ = t, so that the bound on T[U ] is in terms of |U |t . It was
conjectured in [29] that if T satisfies the Convex Wolff axiom with exponents (t, t) then it satisfies the
Furstenberg set estimate.

                                                 4. Uniform structure
   In this section we prove some dyadic versions of pigeonholing from [3]. Let Ωd be the set of triples
(x, t, θ) ∈ [−1, 1]d−1 × [−1, 1] × [−1, 1]d−1 . We think of Ωd as the set of point-line pairs (p ∈ ℓ) in
Rd where p = (x, t) and ℓ = (x, t) + R(θ, 1). Note that not every point-line pair is of this form. The
following proposition allows us to reduce to this form by passing to a large subset of point-line pairs.
Observation 4.1. There exists a set of rigid motions g1 , . . . , gk of Rd with k = Od (1) so that for any
p ∈ [−1, 1]d and any line ℓ through p there exists j such that gj (p) ∈ [−1, 1]d and gj (ℓ) has the form
p + R(θ, 1) for some θ ∈ [−1, 1]d−1 .
  As a corollary, for any collection of point-line pairs (pi ∈ ℓi )N                    d
                                                                   i=1 with pi ∈ [−1, 1] there exists j and
some X ⊂ Ωd such that #{i : ∃(x, t, θ) ∈ X : pi = gj (x, t), ℓi = gj (pi + R(θ, 1))} ⩾ N/k.
  For each (x, t, θ) ∈ Ωd let T ϱ (ω) = D(p), p = (θ, x − tθ)ϱ , be the dyadic ϱ-tube containing the line
(x, t) + R(θ, 1). For dyadic scales v, h, α such that h ⩾ vα and ω0 = (x0 , t0 , θ0 ) ∈ Ωd define
                                                                            (v)                               (α)
                     Rv,h,α (ω0 ) = {ω = (x, t, θ) ∈ Ωd : t ∈ t0 , (x, t) ∈ T h (ω0 ), θ ∈ θ0 }
We view R = Rv,h,α (ω0 ) as a ‘rectangle’ in the parameter space Ωd . Geometrically, it describes the
                                                                                | × .{z
following set of point-line pairs: fix a point-line pair p0 ∈ ℓ0 and consider a h     . . × h} ×v-tube segment
                                                                                                       d−1
Th,v = Th,v (p0 , ℓ0 ) defined by this pair. Then R consists of point-line pairs p ∈ ℓ such that p ∈ Th,v
and ∠(ℓ, ℓ0 ) ⩽ α. Note that if h ⩾ vα then this defines a roughly symmetric relation on the space
of point-line pairs. For example, if h = vα then the condition is essentially that the tube segments
Th,v (p0 , ℓ0 ) and Th,v (p, ℓ) are essentially the same. The variables v, h, α stand for vertical, horizontal
and angular distance, respectively. As in [3], we can consider a net Ωv,h,α   d    of ω0 and define a finitely
overlapping collection of rectangles Rv,h,α covering Ωd . For a configuration X ⊂ Ωd we can then define
a concentration number
                                          MX (v, h, α) = max |R ∩ X|.
                                                                  R∈Rv,h,α

  For parameters K > 0 and ∆1 > . . . > ∆m , let us say that a configuration X ⊂ Ωd is K-uniform
on a sequence of scales ∆1 > . . . > ∆m if for all i, j, k ∈ {1, . . . , m} such that ∆j ⩾ ∆i ∆k and for any
20                                    HONG WANG, DMITRII ZAKHAROV

                                                      1
                                |X ∩ R∆i ,∆j ,∆k (ω0 )| ⩾

   The following lemma was proven (with a slightly different but essentially equivalent definition of
uniformity) in [3] in the two-dimensional case. The higher dimensional version is completely analogous,
so we skip the proof.
                                                                                                  3
Lemma 4.2. Let d ⩾ 2. For any δ > 0 and m ⩾ 1 there exists some K ⩽ (Cd log 1/δ)10m such that
the following holds. For any X ⊂ Ωd and any scales ∆1 > . . . > ∆m ⩾ δ there exists X ′ ⊂ X of size
at least K −1 |X| so that X ′ is K-uniform on scales ∆1 , . . . , ∆m .
  We say that X is (δ, K)-uniform if X is K-uniform along the sequence of scales K −j with j = 0, . . . , m
and m = [logK (1/δ)]. It follows from Lemma 4.2 that we can always find a K-uniform subset X ′ ⊂ X
with K = δ −o(1) (where o(1) is tending to 0 with δ and does not depend on any other parameters),
and this is what we are going to use in applications.
  We can translate between collections of tubes with shadings (T, Y ) and configurations X ⊂ Ωd in
the following way. Given (T, Y ) define X to be the set of triples (x, t, θ) where (x, t) ∈ U (T, Y )δ ∩ (δZ)d
and θ = θ(T ) for some T ∈ TY (p), p = (x, t). In the other direction, for each ω = (x, t, θ) ∈ X consider
the corresponding tube T = T δ (ω) and add the dyadic δ-cube (x, t)δ to the shading of T .

                                 5. Structure of AD-regular sets
   The goal of this section is to prove Lemma 5.3 which finds a useful structure inside an arbitrary
AD-regular set.
   The statement says roughly the following. Let d ⩾ 2 be an integer. Let A ⊂ [−1, 1]d be a (δ, t)-AD-
regular set. Then we can find some intermediate scales ϱ < δ ϵ τ such that (a dense subset of) A ∩ Bτ
is contained in a union of parallel ϱ × τ tubes (radius ϱ, length τ ) T1 , . . . , TM such that:
      • for every i, the set A ∩ Ti is s-AD-regular for some s ∈ [0, t],
      • the set of tubes is (t − s)-AD-regular, and
      • for any other ϱ × τ tube T the set A ∩ T is s-Katz-Tao between scales ϱ and τ .
   Let A ⊂ [0, 1]d be a finite set. For a ∈ A and scales ϱ < τ ⩽ 1 define
                                         Mϱ×τ (a, A) = max |R ∩ A|
                                                            R

where the maximum is over all ϱ × τ tubes R ⊂ R2 centered at a. Let us also define
                                       Mϱ×τ (A) = max Mϱ×τ (a, A).
                                                     a∈A
Up to a constant factor this is the maximum number of points of A contained in a ϱ × τ tube. We will
use the following property later: for any ϱ ⩽ ϱ′ and τ ⩽ τ ′ we have
(24)                             Mϱ′ ×τ ′ (A) ⩽ C(ϱ′ /ϱ)d−1 (τ ′ /τ )Mϱ×τ (A).
  We say that a subset A of some metric space is (δ, K)-uniform if for every r ∈ [δ, 1] and a ∈ A we
have
                                                 1
                                 |A ∩ Br (a)|δ ⩾    max |A ∩ Br (a′ )|δ .
                                                 K a′ ∈A
By a standard dyadic pigeonholing argument, there is some K = δ −o(1) such that any subset A ⊂ [0, 1]d
contains a δ-separated subset A′ ⊂ A of size at least K1 |A|δ which is (δ, K)-uniform.
Proposition 5.1. Let δ > 0 and let A ⊂ [0, 1]d be a δ-separated set. Then for some K = δ −o(1) , there
exists a (δ, K)-uniform subset A′ ⊂ A such that |A′ | ⩾ K1 |A| and
                                                                       
                                                                1             1
                 # a ∈ A′ | ∀ϱ ⩽ τ ∈ [δ, 1] : Mϱ×τ (a, A′ ) ⩾ Mϱ×τ (A′ ) ⩾ |A′ |.
                                                               K              K
                    KAKEYA AND FURSTENBERG PROBLEMS FOR STICKY SETS OF TUBES                                     21

  This proposition shows that the maximal rectangular density centered at each point is about average.

Proof. Let ε > 0 and m = ⌈ε−1 ⌉. Let S = {(i, j) : m ⩾ i > j ⩾ 0} and for a ∈ A define a vector
v(a, A) ∈ ZS so that v(a, A)i,j is equal to the number k for which
                                   Mδi/m ×δj/m (a, A) ∈ [δ −k/m , δ −(k+1)/m ).
                                                                                d
If A ⊂ [0, 1]d is δ-separated then v(a, A) can take at most (2m + 1)m = Oε (1) distinct values. By the
pigeonhole principle we can then find some v1 ∈ NS so that v(a, A) = v1 for at least cε |A| elements
a ∈ A. Let Ã1 ⊂ A be the set of such elements. By pigeonholing, we can find a (δ, Oε (1))-uniform
subset A1 ⊂ Ã1 such that |A1 | ⩾ cε |Ã1 | ⩾ c2ε |A|. Now let v2 be so that v(a, A1 ) = v2 for at least cε |A1 |
elements a ∈ A1 and define the corresponding set Ã2 ⊂ A1 . Find a (δ, Oε (1))-uniform subset A2 ⊂ Ã2
with |A2 | ⩾ cε |Ã2 | ⩾ c2ε |A1 |. Continuing in this manner we define a sequence of vectors v1 , v2 , . . . and
sets A1 ⊃ A2 ⊃ . . . so that |At+1 | ⩾ c2ε |At | and v(a, At ) = vt+1 for any a ∈ At+1 .
   Now note that by At+1 ⊂ At we have vt+2 ⩽ vt+1 coordinate-wise. So since the vectors vt take Oε (1)
distinct values, there exists some t ⩽ Oε (1) for which vt = vt+1 holds. This means that v(a, At ) =
vt+1 = vt for any a ∈ At+1 . On the other hand, for each a ∈ At we have v(a, At ) ⩽ v(a, At−1 ) = vt .
Unpacking definitions, we conclude that
                 n                                                                    o
              # a ∈ At | ∀(i, j) ∈ S : Mδi/m ×δj/m (a, At ) ⩾ δ 1/m Mδi/m ×δj/m (At ) ⩾ cε |At |.

This gives the desired property for pairs ϱ, τ of the form δ i/m , δ j/m . Now let ϱ ∈ [δ i/m , δ (i−1)/m ) and
τ ∈ [δ j/m , δ (j−1)/m ) be arbitrary.
  By (24) we have
                             Mδ(i−1)/m ×δ(j−1)/m (At ) ⩽ Cδ −d/m Mδi/m ×δj/m (At ).
So using the monotonicity we get Mϱ×τ (a, At ) ⩾ Mδi/m ×δj/m (a, At ) and so for cε -fraction of a ∈ At we
get
                         Mϱ×τ (a, At ) ⩾ Mδi/m ×δj/m (a, At ) ⩾ δ 1/m Mδi/m ×δj/m (At )
                         ⩾ cδ (d+1)/m Mδ(i−1)/m ×δ(j−1)/m (At ) ⩾ cδ (d+1)/m Mϱ×τ (At ).

This completes the proof with K = δ −(d+1)/m and sufficiently small δ < δ0 (ε).                                  □

Definition 5.1. We say that a set A ⊂ [−1, 1]d is (δ, t, s, C)-aligned if for some α ∈ [−1, 1]d−1 we can
write
                                A = {(x, y + αx), y ∈ Y, x ∈ Xy },
where Y is (δ, t − s, C)-AD-regular and for each y ∈ Y , Xy ⊂ [−1, 1] is (δ, s, C)-AD-regular. Further-
more, for any ϱ × τ tube T we have |A ∩ T |ϱ ⩽ C(τ /ϱ)s .
   We say that a set A ⊂ [−1, 1]2 is nearly (δ, t, s, C)-aligned if there exists a δ-separated (δ, t, s, C)-
aligned set Ã such that A ⊂ Nδ Ã and Ã ⊂ Nδ A.
  The following lemma requires in addition A to be almost AD-regular.

Lemma 5.3. Let ζ > 0, t ∈ [0, d], then the following holds for η < η0 (ζ), δ < δ0 (η) and χ(ζ) = cζ 4⌈ζ ⌉ .

  In fact, we can take ϕa,τ to be a dilation plus possibly a 90 degree rotation. The rotation is only
needed because Definition 5.1 only allows for slopes α at most 1.

                                   Part 1. Sticky Furstenberg
                                    6. Setup for sticky Furstenberg
  In the following Sections 6–16 we prove Theorem 1.1.
Definition 6.1. We use Fs,t (κ) to denote the following assertion.
  Let s ∈ (0, 1], t ∈ (0, 2]. For any ϵ > 0, there exists η = η(s, t, ϵ) > 0 such that the following holds for
δ > 0 sufficiently small. Let T be a (2t, δ −η )-AD-regular set of δ-tubes and Ct−CW (T) ⩽ δ −η . Suppose
Y is a (δ, s, δ −η )-dense shading on T such that
