                                                                T ∈T
                                        T ∈T
Then
(35)                                     |U (T, Y )|δ ⩾ δ ϵ |Y (T )|δ δ −2t+κ .
   Using this definition, Theorem 1.1 is equivalent to Fs,t (κ) with κ = max{0, t − s, 2t − 2}. To
prove Theorem 1.1 we will choose minimal κ such that Fs,t (κ) fails and study configurations of tubes
which achieve almost equality in (35). In the terminology of [32] these are called extremal families
of
P tubes. Note        that by double counting, a typical point p ∈ U (T, Y ) is contained in approximately
   T |Y (T )| δ −2t |Y (T )|  −κ many shadings Y (T ) (assuming that Y (T ) is a union of dyadic δ-cubes).
 |U (T,Y )| ≈ |U (T,Y )| ≈ δ
Thus, the parameter κ quantifies the multiplicity µ(T, Y ) of the pair (T, Y ).
   The following is a special case of Definition 5.1.
Definition 6.2. We say that a set Θ ⊂ [−1, 1]2 is (δ, κ, γ, C)-aligned if for some α ∈ [−1, 1] we can
write
                                  Θ = {(φ, ξ + αφ), ξ ∈ Ξ, φ ∈ Φξ },
where Ξ is a (δ, κ − γ, C)-AD-regular and for each ξ ∈ Ξ, Φξ is (δ, γ, C)-AD-regular. Furthermore, for
any ϱ × τ tube T we have |Θ ∩ T |ϱ ⩽ C(τ /ϱ)γ .
26                                      HONG WANG, DMITRII ZAKHAROV

  Using induction on scale we reduce the sticky Furstenberg problem to studying the following struc-
tured case.
Definition 6.3. A (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration is the following collection of data:
  (1) A set of δ-tubes T which is (2t, δ −η )-AD-regular. We also assume each tube T ∈ T has θ(T ) ∈
       [−1, 1]2 .
  (2) A δ η -uniform (δ, s)-set Z S⊂ δZ ∩ [−1, 1].
  (3) Set E of the form E = z∈Z Ez where Ez = Az × {z} and Az ⊂ [−1, 1]2 is a (δ, 2t − κ, δ −η )-
       AD-regular set.
  (4) For every p ∈ E, there is a δ-separated (δ, κ, γ, δ −η )-aligned set Θp ⊂ [−1, 1]2 such that: for
       every θ ∈ Θp there exists T ∈ T with p ∈ T and θ(T ) ∈ Nδ θ. We denote by Tp ⊂ T the set of
       all such tubes over θ ∈ Θp .
  For short, we represent this data by the tuple C = (T, E, {Tp }). A λ-refinement of a Furstenberg
configuration is a Furstenberg configuration C ′ = (T, E ′ , {T′p }) with E ′ ⊂ E and T′p ⊂ Tp and
                                         X              X
                                             |T′p | ⩾ λ       |Tp |.
                                             p∈E ′          p∈E

Note that we always keep the set of tubes the same.
Remark 6.1. For each T ∈ T, let Z(T ) be the set of z ∈ Z so that T ∈ Tp for some p ∈ Ez . Then
                                  X
                                       |Z(T )|δ ≳ δ 4η |T|δ |Z|δ .
                                           T ∈T

To see this, by Item (3), |E|δ ≳ δ η |Z|δ δ −(2t−κ) . By Item (4) and double counting,
                     X
                         |Z(T )|δ ∼ |E|δ |Θp |δ ≳ |Z|δ δ −(2t−κ) · δ −κ+2η ≳ δ 4η |T|δ |Z|δ ,
                       T ∈T

where the last inequality is because T is a (2t, δ −η )-AD-regular set.
  We prove Theorem 1.1 using the following four propositions. Note that only Proposition 6.5 requires
the Convex Wolff axiom to obtain a desired bound on κ.
  The first proposition uses critical pairs (T, Y ), i.e. which nearly achieve equality in (35), to construct
Furstenberg configurations with the same parameter κ and some γ ∈ [0, κ].
Proposition 6.2. Let κ be the infimum of the set of κ′ such that Fs,t (κ′ ) holds. Then for any δ0 , η
there exists δ < δ0 and γ ∈ [0, κ] so that there exists a (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration whose
set T of δ-tubes satisfies Ct−CW (T) ⩽ δ −η .
     The following propositions give upper bounds on κ for different ranges of the parameter γ.
Proposition 6.3. For any ϵ0 > 0, there exists η > 0 such that the following holds for δ > 0 sufficiently
small.
  Suppose that there is a (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration. If γ < s + κ − 1 − ϵ0 then κ ⩽
max{2t − 2, 0} + 2ϵ0 .
Proposition 6.4. For any ϵ0 > 0, ϵ1 > 0, there exists η > 0 such that the following holds for δ > 0
sufficiently small.
   Suppose there is a (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration. Suppose that γ ∈ [s+κ−1−ϵ0 , 1−s+ϵ0 ]
and γ ∈ [ϵ1 , κ − ϵ1 ] then κ ⩽ max{t − s, 0} + 4ϵ0 .
Proposition 6.5. For any ϵ0 > 0, there exists η > 0 such that the following holds for δ > 0 sufficiently
small.
   Suppose there is a (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration whose set of δ-tubes T satisfies Ct−CW (T) ⩽
δ −η . If γ ⩾ max{s + κ − 1 − ϵ0 , 1 − s + ϵ0 } then κ ⩽ max{2t − 2, t − s, 0} + 4ϵ0 .
                     KAKEYA AND FURSTENBERG PROBLEMS FOR STICKY SETS OF TUBES                                          27

  The three propositions above roughly consider different cases of how γ relates to the numbers s+κ−1
and 1 − s, except for the fact that Proposition 6.4 in addition requires that γ is separated from the
edges of the interval [0, κ]. The following two propositions are aimed at covering those two edge cases.
Proposition 6.6. For any ϵ0 > 0, there exists η > 0 such that the following holds for δ > 0 sufficiently
small.
   Suppose there is a (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration whose set of δ-tubes T satisfies Ct−CW (T) ⩽
δ −η . If γ ⩽ ϵ0 and s + κ ⩽ 1 + ϵ0 then κ ⩽ max{2t − 2, t − s, 0} + 2ϵ0 .
Proposition 6.7. For any ϵ0 > 0 and sufficiently small ϵ1 > 0, there exists η > 0 such that the
following holds for δ > 0 sufficiently small.
   Suppose there is a (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration whose set of δ-tubes T satisfies Ct−CW (T) ⩽
δ −η . If γ ⩾ κ − ϵ1 and s + κ ⩽ 1 + ϵ0 then κ ⩽ max{2t − 2, t − s, 0} + 2ϵ0 .
  Proposition 6.2 is proved in Section 8. Proposition 6.3 is proved in Section 9. Proposition 6.4 is
proved in Section 10. Proposition 6.5 is proved in Sections 11–14. Proposition 6.6 is proved in Section
15 and Proposition 6.7 is proved in Section 16.
Proof of Theorem 1.1. Note that the statement of Theorem 1.1 is equivalent to the assertion Fs,t (κ0 )
where κ0 = max(2t − 2, t − s, 0). Let κ be the infimum of all κ′ such that Fs,t (κ′ ) holds. It suffices
to show that κ ⩽ κ0 . Let ϵ0 > 0 be an arbitrarily small constant, let ϵ1 = ϵ1 (2ϵ0 ) be constant from
Proposition 6.7 and let η be sufficiently small depending on ϵ0 , ϵ1 .
   By Proposition 6.2, for any δ0 there is δ < δ0 and γ ∈ [0, κ] so that there exists a (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-
Furstenberg configuration whose set of δ-tubes T satisfies Ct−CW (T) ⩽ δ −η . Then
      • if γ ⩽ s + κ − 1 − ϵ0 , then by Proposition 6.3 we have κ ⩽ max(2t − 2, 0) + 2ϵ0 ⩽ κ0 + 2ϵ0 ,
      • if γ ∈ [s+κ−1−ϵ0 , 1−s+ϵ0 ]∩[ϵ1 , κ−ϵ1 ] then by Proposition 6.4 we have κ ⩽ max(t−s, 0)+4ϵ0 ⩽
          κ0 + 4ϵ0 ,
      • if γ ⩾ max{s + κ − 1 − ϵ0 , 1 − s + ϵ0 } then by Proposition 6.5 we have κ ⩽ max{2t − 2, t −
          s, 0} + 4ϵ0 ⩽ κ0 + 4ϵ0 ,
      • if κ ⩽ 1 − s + 2ϵ0 and γ ⩽ ϵ1 then by Proposition 6.6 we have κ ⩽ max{2t − 2, t − s, 0} + 4ϵ0 .
      • if κ ⩽ 1 − s + 2ϵ0 and γ ⩾ κ − ϵ1 then by Proposition 6.7 we have κ ⩽ max{2t − 2, t − s, 0} + 4ϵ0 .
   This exhausts all possibilities for γ: the first three bullet points cover everything except γ ∈ [s + κ −
1 − ϵ0 , 1 − s + ϵ0 ] ∩ [0, ϵ0 ] and γ ∈ [s + κ − 1 − ϵ0 , 1 − s + ϵ0 ] ∩ [κ − ϵ0 , κ]. If the first interval intersection
is non-empty, then s + κ − 1 − ϵ0 ⩽ ϵ0 and the 4-th bullet applies; if the second interval intersection is
non-empty, then 1 − s + ϵ0 ⩾ κ − ϵ0 and the 5-th bullet applies.
   We conclude that in all cases we have κ ⩽ κ0 + 4ϵ0 . Since ϵ0 > 0 is arbitrary, we conclude that
κ ⩽ κ0 holds, concluding the proof.                                                                                      □

6.1. Using weaker Convex Wolff axioms. By remarks at the end of Sections 14 and 16, the sharp
sticky Fustenberg estimate holds with a slightly relaxed convex Wolff axiom Ct;t′ −CW (T) ⩽ δ −η for
some t′ ⩽ t′ (s, t) that can be defined as follows:
                                     
                                     
                                      min(2t, s), t ∈ [0, s],
                                     
                                     2t − s, t ∈ [s, 1],
                                t′ =
                                     
                                      2 − s, t ∈ [1, 2 − s],
                                     
                                       min(2t − 2 + s, 2), t ∈ [2 − s, 2].
                                     

Recall that in the definition of Ct;t′ −CW we require that t′ ∈ [0, 2] and t′ ⩽ 2t (so that both exponents
        ′ ′
in a2t−t bt are non-negative), hence the second terms in the two minimums above. It turns out that
all other restrictions on t′ are in fact necessary, as we show on the following two examples.
   For some t1 ⩽ 2t let (T1 , Y1 ) be a sharp example for Furstenberg in the plane with T1 a (δ, t1 )-AD-
regular and Y1 (T ) a (δ, s)-set. Take a A ⊂ [−1, 1] a (δ, 2t − t1 )-AD-regular set and let T be the union
28                                      HONG WANG, DMITRII ZAKHAROV

of copies of T1 inside the planes [−1, 1]2 × {a}, a ∈ A. Then T is almost 2t-AD-regular and
                                    µ(T) ≳ µ(T1 ) ≳ δ − max(0,(t1 −s)/2,t1 −1) .
It is straightforward to check that Ct;t1 −CW (T) ⪅ 1: indeed, the worst case is U = [−1, 1]2 × [−δ, δ] for
which |T[U ]| ≈ |T1 | ≈ δ −t1 and this should be upper bounded by δ 2t (1/δ)t1 |T| ≈ δ −t1 .
   Consider different cases:
       • If t ∈ [0, s], put t1 = s + ζ, so µ(T) ≫ 1 = δ −κ0 (s,t) . Thus, we need t′ ⩽ s.
       • If t ∈ [s, 1], put t1 = 2t − s + ζ, so µ(T) ≫ δ −(t−s) = δ −κ0 (s,t) . Thus, we need t′ ⩽ 2t − s.
       • If t ∈ [1, 2 − s], put t1 = t + 1 − s + ζ, so µ(T) ≫ δ −(t−s) = δ −κ0 (s,t) . Thus, we need t′ ⩽ t + 1 − s.
   For t ∈ [2 − s, 2] we will give a better example. Again let (T1 , Y1 ) be a planar Furstenberg pair
so that T1 is almost (2t − t1 )-AD-regular for some t1 and Y is s-set. Suppose that t1 ∈ [2 − s, 2].
For each T1 ∈ T1 let TT1 be a (δ, t1 )-AD-regularSset of tubes in T × [−1, 1]. Define the shading
by Y (T ) = T ∩ (Y1 (T1 ) × [−1, 1]) and put T = T1 ∈T1 TT1 . Note that for t1 ∈ [2 − s, 2] we have
U (TT1 , Y ) ≈ Y (T1 ) × [−1, 1] and µ(TT1 ) ≈ δ −(t1 −1) .
   Then T is almost 2t-AD-regular and
                         µ(T) ≳ µ(T1 )µ(TT1 ) ≳ δ − max(0,(2t−t1 −s)/2,2t−t1 −1) δ −(t1 −1) .
As previously, we can verify that Ct;t1 (T) ⪅ 1. So
       • If t ∈ [1, 2 − s], put t1 = 2 − s + ζ, so µ(T) ≫ δ −(t−s) = δ −κ0 (s,t) . Thus, we need t′ ⩽ 2 − s.
       • If t ∈ [2 − s, 2], put t1 = 2t − 2 + s + ζ, so µ(T) ≫ δ −(2t−2) = δ −κ0 (s,t) . Thus, we need
         t′ ⩽ 2t − 2 + s.

                           E(pϱ0 ) = {p ∈ E ∩ pϱ0 : |Θp ∩ Θϱp′ |δ ⩾ δ C0 η |Θp |δ }
                                                             0

has size at least δ O(η) |E ∩ pϱ0 | (for an appropriately large constant C0 ). Taking E ′ to be the union
of sets E(pϱ0 ) over all p0 representing distinct dyadic ϱ boxes gives (a). Part (b) follows from (a) by
simple double counting.                                                                                 □
  The following lemma will be applied to the shading Z to find scales β so that Z ∩ z (β) is a rescaled
(β, s, β −ε )-set. Essentially the same statement appears in [21, Proposition 4.32].
Lemma 7.3. For any ϵ > 0 and s ∈ (0, 1] there exists µ, η0 , δ0 > 0 such that the following holds for
all η < η0 and δ < δ0 . Let A ⊂ [0, 1] be a (δ, s, δ −η )-set and suppose that A is (δ, δ −η )-uniform. Then
there exists β ∈ [δ 1/2 , δ µ ] such that A ∩ B(a, β) is a rescaled (β, s, β −ϵ )-set for every a ∈ A. That is,
for every a ∈ A and ϱ ∈ [β 2 , β] we have
                                |A ∩ B(a, ϱ)|β 2 ⩽ β −ϵ (ϱ/β)s |A ∩ B(a, β)|β 2 .
Proof. Let f : [0, 1] → [0, 1] be the branching function of A, i.e.
                                                 |A|δx = δ −f (x) .
Note that since A is (δ, δ −η )-uniform, f (x) is a meaningful function up to precision η. Since A is
(δ, s, δ −η )-set we have
                                          f (x) ⩾ sx − η − o(1)
                                                              c
where o(1) tends to 0 with δ → 0 (in fact, it decays as log(1/δ) ). This term comes from a constant
factor loss which occurs when we compare the definition of covering number using dyadic intervals and
the Frostman set condition using neighborhoods.
30                                     HONG WANG, DMITRII ZAKHAROV

   Since for any ϱ ⩽ τ we have |A|ϱ ⩽ |A|τ ⩽ C(τ /ϱ)|A|ϱ , we also know that f (x) ⩽ f (x + h) ⩽
f (x) + h + o(1) for any x + h ⩽ 1. Let g(x) = f (x) − sx, so that g(x) ⩾ −η + o(1) for all x ∈ [0, 1] and
g is a 1-Lipschitz function (up to precision o(1)). We say that a scale b ∈ [0, 1/2] is ϵ-good if
                                           f (b + x) ⩾ f (b) + xs − ϵb
for all x ∈ [0, b]. Equivalently, b is ϵ-good if
                                               g(x) ⩾ g(b) − ϵb/2
for every x ∈ [b, 2b].
   Let µ = 2−1−M , where M = ⌈4ϵ−1 ⌉ and suppose that η ⩽ µ/2. Suppose there is no ϵ-good scale
b ∈ [µ, 1/2]. Then we can let b0 = µ and construct a sequence bi , i = 0, 1, . . ., such that for i ⩾ 0:
      • bi+1 ⩽ 2bi ,
      • g(bi+1 ) ⩽ g(bi ) − ϵbi /2.
   Thus, after M steps we obtain some bM ⩽ 2M µ ⩽ 1/2 and
                                                                                                      M
                                                                                                      X −1
       g(bM ) ⩽ g(bM −1 ) − (ϵ/2)bM −1 ⩽ g(bM −2 ) − (ϵ/2)(bM −1 + bM −2 ) ⩽ . . . ⩽ g(b0 ) − (ϵ/2)          bj
                                                                                                       j=0

Now we use g(b0 ) ⩽ b0 + o(1) and bj ⩾ b0 to conclude that
                   −η + o(1) ⩽ g(bM ) ⩽ b0 − (ϵ/2)M b0 + o(1) ⩽ −b0 + o(1) ⩽ −µ + o(1)
and so for sufficiently small δ we obtain a contradiction with the condition η ⩽ µ/2.
  Thus, there is a good scale b ∈ [µ, 1/2]. Unraveling the definition, it follows that for every x ∈ [0, b]:
                                           |A|δb+x ⩾ δ ϵb/2 δ −xs |A|δb .
Let β = δ b . Since A is (δ, δ −η )-uniform, this implies that A ∩ B(a, β) is a rescaled (β, s, Cβ −ϵ/2 δ −η )-set.
For small enough η and δ ⩽ δ0 , we have Cδ −η < δ −ϵµ/2 ⩽ β −ϵ/2 giving the result.                              □

         8. Construction of Furstenberg configurations: Proof of Proposition 6.2
  Let κ be the infimum of all κ′ such that Fs,t (κ′ ) holds. Then for any ν > 0, Fs,t (κ − ν) does not
hold. Therefore, for every ν > 0 there exists ϵ > 0 such that for all sufficiently small η > 0 and δ > 0,
there exists a (2t, δ −η )-AD-regular set of δ-tubes with Ct−CW (T) ⩽ δ −η and a (δ, s, δ −η )-shading Y on
T with |Y (T )|δ ∼ M for every T ∈ T and
                                  |U (T, Y )|δ ⩽ δ ϵ−ν M δ −2t+κ ⩽ M δ −2t+κ−ν .
Since the properties of Furstenberg configurations are monotone in η, we may replace the parameter η
by max(η, ν), i.e. without loss of generality,
(37)                                      |U (T, Y )|δ ⩽ δ −η M δ −2t+κ .
Equivalently,

We are going to show that for any δ0 and η0 , there exists δ1 < δ0 and γ ∈ [0, κ] so that there exists
a (δ1 , η0 , s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration. We are going to take η, δ sufficiently small compared to
η0 , δ0 , respectively.
Step 1. Define point line configuration and obtain uniform refinement.
   By using Proposition 3.1 we can replace T by a large subset and increase δ slightly so that the

provided that ϵ1 , η ≪ η0 are small enough.

                           9. High-low estimate: Proof of Proposition 6.3
  In the following proposition we use high-low method to prove a restricted projection estimate in the
case when the set of directions Θ has strong non-concentration in rectangles. This result is similar to
34                                                   HONG WANG, DMITRII ZAKHAROV

the incidence estimate [8, Proposition 2.1]. The main difference is that the restriction on the direction
set Θ allows us to more effectively estimate the high frequency part.
Proposition 9.1. Let α ∈ (0, 1). Then for any ε > 0 the following holds for η ⩽ η0 (ε, α) and
δ ⩽ δ0 (η, α).
   Let Θ ⊂ [−1, 1]2 be a δ-separated set such that for any δ × 1 tube T we have |Θ ∩ T | ⩽ ν|Θ| for some
ν ∈ (0, 1). For each θ ∈ Θ let Aθ ⊂ [−1, 1]2 be a δ-separated set of size ∼ M for some M ⩾ 1 and such
that |Aθ ∩ BSδ | ⩽ δ −η S 2−α for any S ⩾ 1. For each a ∈ Aθ let E(a, θ) ⊂ ((a, 0) + R(θ, 1)) ∩ [−1, 1]3 be
a δ-separated set of size ∼ λδ −1 for some λ ∈ (0, 1). Let E be the union of E(a, θ) over all a ∈ Aθ and
all θ ∈ Θ and suppose that |E|δ ⩽ δ −η M λδ −1 .
   Then we have λ ⩽ δ −ε ν.

                   10. Intermediate tube density: Proof of Proposition 6.4
  In this section, we prove Proposition 6.4. The main tool is Orponen-Shmerkin discretized ABC-sum
production theorem.
Theorem 10.1 (Orponen-Shmerkin Theorem 1.7 [21]). Let 0 < β ⩽ α < 1. Then, for every γ ∈
(α − β, 1], there exist χ, δ0 ∈ (0, 1/2] such that the following holds. Let δ ∈ 2−N with δ ∈ (0, δ0 ], and
let A, B ⊂ δ · Z ∩ [0, 1] be sets satisfying the following hypotheses:
    (1) |A| ⩽ δ −α ,
    (2) B ̸= ∅, and |B ∩ B(x, r)| ⩽ δ −χ rβ |B| for all x ∈ R and r ∈ [δ, 1].
Further, let C ⊂ δ · Z ∩ [−1, 1] be a non-empty set satisfying |C ∩ B(x, r)| ⩽ δ −χ rγ |C| for x ∈ R and
r ∈ [δ, 1]. Then there exists c ∈ C such that
(58)                 |{a + cb : (a, b) ∈ G}|δ ⩾ δ −χ |A|,         G ⊂ A × B, |G| ⩾ δ χ |A||B|.
Remark 10.2. Note that our statement has C ⊂ [−1, 1] instead of C ⊂ [1, 2] in [21]. This version
follows from exactly the same proof and a version with slightly different error terms in place of various
δ −χ follows directly from their result.

                                       11. Global grains reduction
   In this section and the following sections we prove Proposition 6.5. Recall that in this proposition
we assume that γ ⩾ max(s + κ − 1 − ϵ0 , 1 − s + ϵ0 ) for a small fixed ϵ0 > 0. In this section we apply
rescaling and thickening operations to reduce to the case when the set E has ‘global grains’ structure
(see Section 2 to compare with the 3D sticky Kakeya case).
Definition 11.1. Let Z ⊂ [−1, 1] and let f : Z → [−1, 1]m be a function. We say that (Z, f ) is
(δ, K)-uniform if for any ϱ, σ ∈ [δ, 1] there is some number Mϱ,σ such that for every z ∈ Z:
                     #{z ′ ∈ Z : |z − z ′ | ⩽ ϱ, |f (z) − f (z ′ )| ⩽ σ} ∈ [K −1 Mϱ,σ , Mϱ,σ ].
   By standard pigeonholing, for any δ there is some K = δ o(1) such that for any Z and f we can find
a δ-separated subset Z ′ ⊂ Z such that |Z ′ | ⩾ K −1 |Z|δ and (Z ′ , f |Z ′ ) is (δ, K)-uniform.
   The next definition is a modification of Definition 6.3, where the set E has additional structure
called ‘global grains’.
Definition 11.2. Let t ∈ [0, 2], s ∈ [0, 1], κ, γ ⩾ 0 so that 2t ⩾ 1 + κ.
 A global grains (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration is the following collection of data:
  (1) A (2t, δ −η )-AD-regular set of tubes T.
  (2) A δ-separated (δ, s)-set Z ⊂ [−1, 1] and a function f : Z → [−1, 1] such that (Z, f ) is (δ, δ −η )-
       uniform.
  (3) A δ-separated set E of the form
                     G
                E=        Ez = {(x, y + f (z)x, z), z ∈ Z, y ∈ Yz , x ∈ Xy,z } ⊂ [−1, 1]3 ,
                        z∈Z

         where Yz ⊂ [−1, 1] is a (δ, 2t − 1 − κ, δ −η )-AD-regular set and Xy,z ⊂ [−1, 1] is such that
         |Xy,z |δ ⩾ δ η−1 .
     (4) For each p ∈ Ez , some δ-separated (δ, κ − γ, δ −η )-AD-regular set Ξp ⊂ [−1, 1] and δ-separated
         (δ, γ, δ −η )-AD-regular sets Φp,ξ ⊂ [−1, 1] such that for any ξ ∈ Ξp and φ ∈ Φp,ξ there is a tube
         T ∈ T through p with θ(T ) ∈ Nδ (φ, ξ + f (z)φ).
                   KAKEYA AND FURSTENBERG PROBLEMS FOR STICKY SETS OF TUBES                                 39

  The following lemma is a variant of Lemma 7.2 adapted to the global grains configurations.
Lemma 11.1. Let (T, E, {Tp }) be a global grains (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration. Let ϱ ∈

         |T(q)| ∼δO(η) ϱ−κ (ϱ/δ)2t . For T ∈ T(q) let Z(q, T ) be the set of z ∈ Z(q) such that T ∈ Tp for

                                      X

                                   T ∈T(q)

   (c) We can write
                     E(q) = {(x, y + f (z)x, z), z ∈ Z(q), y ∈ Yz (q), x ∈ Xy,z (q)},
        where Yz (q) is a rescaled (δ/ϱ, 2t − κ − 1, δ −O(η) )-AD-regular set and Xz (q) is a rescaled

Proof. Properties (a) and (b) are the content of Lemma 7.2. To obtain (c) we use property (3) in the

are AD-regular.                                                                                        □
11.1. Constructing global grains. Suppose we have a (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration and
that s + γ ⩾ 1. We use this structure to construct a global grains configuration (δ̃, η̃, s, t, κ, γ). We
accomplish this in the following two steps. First, we show that after rescaling each set Az has the
global grains structure. Second, after another rescaling we show that the direction sets Θp are aligned
with global grains.
Proposition 11.2 (Global grains, step 1). Suppose that γ ⩾ 1 − s and suppose that for any η and
δ0 there is δ < δ0 so that a (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration exists. Then for any δ0 , η there is

By construction, we now have Ẽ = z∈Z̃ Ãz × {z} and sets Ãz have the global grains structure with

and has the form (61), Z̃ is ϱ−ϵ -uniform and Θp̃ is (ϱ, κ, γ, ϱ−ϵ )-aligned. So we obtain a configuration
satisfying Definition 6.3 and the global grain property stated in the lemma.                                 □
Proposition 11.3 (Global grains, step 2). Suppose that γ ⩾ 1−s and that 2t−κ ⩽ 2−ϵ0 for some fixed
ϵ0 > 0. Suppose that for any η and δ0 there is δ < δ0 so that a (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration
exists. Then for any δ0 , η there is δ < δ0 so that there exists a global grains (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg
configuration.
Proof. We start with a (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration satisfying the global grains property
from Proposition 11.2. Out goal is to produce a global grains Fustenberg configuration with error
η0 . Suppose that η ≪ η0 is sufficiently small. By dyadic pigeonholing we can find some τ ∈ [δ, 1]
and a subset E ′ ⊂ E so that |αp − f (z)| ∼ τ (or ⩽ δ if τ = δ) for every p ∈ E ′ ∩ Ez and we have
|E ′ | ≳ log(1/δ)
             1
                  |E|.
                   KAKEYA AND FURSTENBERG PROBLEMS FOR STICKY SETS OF TUBES                                   41

   Fix some small ε > 0. First suppose that we have τ ⩽ δ ε . In this case we thicken the set of tubes
T and points E ′ to scale Cτ to obtain a new (Cτ, O(η/ε), s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration (which
still has global grains from Proposition 11.2) and redefine αp = f (z). Since |αp − f (z)| ≲ τ , the set of
directions Θp can be slightly modified to satisfy property (4) from Definition 11.2. So in this case we
get the desired conclusion provided that η/ε ≪ η0 .
   Pick some ε ≪ η0 and suppose that τ ⩾ δ ε holds. Let ϱ ∈ [δ 1/2 , δ µ ] be the scale given by Lemma 7.3
so that Z ∩ z is rescaled (ϱ, s, ϱ−η0 )-set for z = z ϱ (here µ = µ(η0 )). Suppose that ε is small enough in
terms of η0 so that ε ≪ µ. Choose some p0 ∈ E and let q = pϱ0 and run the argument in Proposition
11.2 inside q. By repeating the proof, we get that after some pigeonholing the set Az (q) for z ∈ Z(q)
satisfies
                           Az (q) ⊂ Nϱ2 {(x, y + αq x), y ∈ Yz (q), x ∈ Xy,z (q)}
where we have |Xy,z (q)|ϱ2 ⩾ ϱη0 −1 and Yz (q) is rescaled (ϱ, 2t − κ − 1, ϱ−O(η0 ) δ −O(η) )-set. On the other
hand, we know from global grains that Az (q) ⊂ Az and
                                  Az = {(x, y + f (z)x), x ∈ Xy,z , y ∈ Yz }
where |Xy,z |δ ⩾ δ η−1 and Yz is (δ, 2t − κ − 1, δ −η )-AD-regular set. By restricting to E ∩ q, we then can
construct cϱ2 -separated sets X̃y,z (q) and Ỹ (q) such that
                           Az (q) ⊂ Nϱ2 {(x, y + f (z)x), x ∈ X̃y,z (q), y ∈ Ỹz (q)}
where we have |X̃y,z (q)| ⩾ δ O(η) ϱ−1 and Ỹz (q) is rescaled (ϱ, 2t − κ − 1, δ −O(η) )-AD-regular set.
  This gives us upper bounds on orthogonal projections
                      |π(1,αq ) (Az (q))|ϱ2 , |π(1,f (z)) (Az (q))|ϱ2 ⩽ ϱ−O(η0 ) δ −O(η) ϱκ+1−2t
and so using the lower bound |αq − f (z)| ⩾ τ (recall that when we apply Lemma 7.2 we can choose
αq = αp for a typical p ∈ E ∩ q) this implies that
                          |Az (q)|ϱ2 ≲ τ −1 |π(1,αq ) (Az (q))|ϱ2 · |π(1,f (z)) (Az (q))|ϱ2
                                  ϱκ−2t ≲ τ −1 ϱ−O(η0 ) δ −O(η) ϱ2(κ+1−2t)
Recall that τ ⩾ ϱε so this implies that
                                    2t − κ ⩽ 2(2t − κ − 1) + O(ε + η/µ).
Since by the assumption we have 2t − κ ⩽ 2 − ϵ0 , this gives a contradiction for small enough ε, η.
We conclude that the alternative τ ⩽ δ ε must hold and we get the desired global grains Furstenberg
configuration.                                                                                   □
   In the next lemma we show that we may assume the function f is dyadic Lipschitz on a given scale
ϱ, provided that κ is larger than some threshold.
Lemma 11.4. Consider a global grains (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration with slope function f .
Let ϱ ∈ [δ 1/4 , δ µ ] be such that Z ∩ z, z = z ϱ is rescaled (ϱ, s, ϱ−ϵ )-set. Suppose that 2t − κ − 1 ⩽ 1 − ζ
for some ζ > 0. Then there is a subset Z̃ ⊂ Z ∩ z of density δ O(η/ζ) such that |f (z1 ) − f (z2 )| ⩽ ϱ for
all z1 , z2 ∈ Z̃.
Proof. Apply Lemma 11.1 and fix a cube q = pϱ0 on the same level as z. For z ∈ Z(q) let us write
(62)                       Ez (q) = {(x, y + f (z)x, z), x ∈ Xy,z (q), y ∈ Yz (q)}
for rescaled (δ/ϱ, 2t − 1 − κ, δ −O(η) )-AD-regular set Yz (q) and (δ/ϱ, 1, δ −O(η) )-AD-regular sets Xy,z (q).
We have |Θp ∩ Θϱq | ⩾ δ O(η) |Θp | for all p ∈ E(q). By pigeonhole, we can find ϱ-dyadic cube θ ⊂ Θϱq so
that Θp ∩ θ ̸= ∅ for at least δ O(η) |E(q)|-many p ∈ E(q). Since Tp is (δ, κ, δ −η )-AD-regular, we get
                                      X
                                              |Z(q, T )| ⩾ δ O(η) |Z(q)|(ϱ/δ)2t .
                                T ∈T(q):θ(T )∈θ

It follows from Cauchy–Schwarz that
                   X

                     z,z ′ ∈Z(q)

i.e. up to resolution O(ϱ2 ), the layers Ez (q) have essentially the same projection in direction (θ, 1)
(here we abuse notation and identify the cube θ with its middle point).
   On the other hand, by (62) we have

                        π(θ,1) (Ez (q)) = {(x, y + f (z)x) − θz,        x ∈ Xy,z (q), y ∈ Yz (q)}.

Denote τ = max(|f (z) − f (z ′ )|, ϱ) for some fixed z, z ′ ∈ Z(q). Cover the projection π(θ,1) (Ez (q)) by
∼ |Yz (q)|τ ϱ -many τ ϱ × ϱ tubes Tj . Then inside each tube we have a bound

        |Tj ∩ π(θ,1) (Ez (q)) ∩ NCϱ2 π(θ,1) (Ez ′ (q))|ϱ2 ≲ δ −O(η) τ −1 |Yz (q) ∩ Nτ ϱ y|ϱ2 |Yz ′ (q) ∩ Nτ ϱ y ′ |ϱ2

where y ∈ Yz (q) and y ′ ∈ Yz ′ (q) are chosen so that the corresponding lines (x, y + f (z)x) and (x′ , y ′ +
f (z ′ )x′ ) are contained in the tube Tj . Indeed, each ϱ2 -ball Bϱ2 intersecting the set on the left hand side
is contained in a ϱ2 × ϱ2 /τ rectangle R proportional to Tj . This rectangle projects onto ∼ ϱ2 -length
intervals I1 , I2 by the maps (a, b) → (a, b − f (z)a) and (a, b) → (a, b − f (z ′ )a) and R is essentially
uniquely determined by I1 , I2 . Now there are τ −1 -many ways to select Bϱ2 ⊂ R and by definition we
have I1 ⊂ NCϱ2 Yz (q) and I2 ⊂ NCϱ2 Yz ′ (q), giving the bound above.
   So summing over Tj gives

     |π(θ,1) (Ez (q)) ∩ NCϱ2 π(θ,1) (Ez ′ (q))|ϱ2 ≲ δ −O(η) (τ −1 |Yz (q)|ϱτ )|Yz (q) ∩ Nτ ϱ y|ϱ2 |Yz ′ (q) ∩ Nτ ϱ y ′ |ϱ2

where we used that Yz (q) is (δ/ϱ, 2t − κ − 1, δ O(η) )-AD-regular. It follows from (63) that
                          X
                                min(1, ϱ/|f (z) − f (z ′ )|)2+κ−2t ⩾ δ O(η) |Z(q)|2 .
                               z,z ′ ∈Z(q)

By assumption, we have 2 + κ − 2t ⩾ ζ. We conclude that some constant C and for any S ⩾ 1 we have

#{(z, z ′ ) ∈ Z(q) × Z(q) : |f (z) − f (z ′ )| ⩽ Sϱ} + #{(z, z ′ ) ∈ Z(q) × Z(q) : |f (z) − f (z ′ )| > Sϱ}S −ζ
                                                                                                             > δ Cη |Z(q)|2 .

So by taking S = δ −2Cη/ζ we conclude

                      #{(z, z ′ ) ∈ Z(q) × Z(q) : |f (z) − f (z ′ )| ⩽ δ −Cη/ζ ϱ} ≳ δ Cη |Z(q)|2 .

Thus, by pigeonholing we can find the desired set Z̃ ⊂ Z(q) on which |f (z) − f (z ′ )| ⩽ ϱ, completing
the proof.                                                                                            □


                        12. Linear and small variation of the slope function.
   In this section we analyze two special types of global grains Furstenberg configuration. The first
is the linear slope function case: f (z) = az + b + O(ϱ1+ε ) for z in a fixed ϱ-interval and a ≳ 1. The
second is small variation case: |f (z) − f (z ′ )| ≲ ϱ1+ε for z, z ′ in the same ϱ-interval.
                   KAKEYA AND FURSTENBERG PROBLEMS FOR STICKY SETS OF TUBES                                 43

12.1. W -tuples. The tube relation. Our analysis is based on counting paths in the Furstenberg con-
figuration (T, E, {Tp }). For tubes T1 , T2 ∈ T and p ∈ Ez , write T1 ↔ T2 if T1 , T2 ∈ Tp and there are
                                                                                 p
ξ ∈ Ξp and φ1 , φ2 ∈ Φp,ξ such that
                                   θ(Ti ) ∈ Nδ (φi , ξ + f (z)φi ),       i = 1, 2.
Thus the two tubes belong to the same γ-dimensional direction fiber at p. In particular,
(64)                              #{(p, T1 , T2 ) : T1 ↔ T2 } ∼δO(η) |E|δ −κ−γ .
                                                          p

Indeed, after choosing p, there are δ −κ+O(η) choices for T1 . Its direction determines ξ up to O(1)
choices, and the fiber Φp,ξ gives δ −γ+O(η) choices for T2 .
Observation 12.1. Suppose that T1 ↔ T2 for some p ∈ Ez and let φi be the first coordinate of θ(Ti ).
                                              p
Then
(65)                             θ(T1 ) − θ(T2 ) = (φ1 − φ2 )(1, f (z)) + O(δ).
Proof. By definition, we have some ξ ∈ Ξp and φ̃i ∈ Φp,ξ so that θ(Ti ) ∈ Nδ (φ̃i , ξ + f (z)φ̃i ). It follows
that |φi − φ̃i | ⩽ δ and so subtracting gives the result.                                                   □

   Fix a scale ϱ ∈ [δ, 1] and a dyadic cube q = pϱ0 . We use the refinement E(q) ⊂ E ∩ q, together with
Z(q) and T(q), given by Lemma 11.1. Using Lemma 11.4, we can refine Z(q) so that |f (z) − f (z ′ )| ≲ ϱ
for all z, z ′ ∈ Z(q) (unless κ ⩾ 2t − 2 − ζ in which case we obtain a satisfactory lower bound on κ).

Additive relations. Fix a tuple (p, p1 , p2 , p′1 , p′2 , T1 , T2 , T1′ , T2′ ) in Wz,z ′ ,z ′′ (q), and let φi , φ′i be the first

Since a W -tuple satisfies |θ(T1′ ) − θ(T2′ )| ⩽ ϱ, we have |φ′1 − φ′2 | ⩽ ϱ. The last display therefore implies
the following two estimates that will be used later:
(71)                              x′1 − x′2 = (z ′ − z)(φ1 − φ2 ) + O(ϱ2 + δ),
                                  y1′ − y2′ = O ϱ|f (z) − f (z ′ )| + ϱ|f (z ′ ) − f (z ′′ )| + δ .

12.2. Linear slope function. In this section we consider the case when f can be locally approximated
by a linear function.
Lemma 12.4. For any ζ, µ > 0, η0 ∈ (0, 1/2) the following holds for all η < η(ζ, µ, η0 ) and ϵ < ϵ(ζ, η0 ).
   Consider a global grains (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration with s+γ ⩾ 1−ζ/4 and let f : Z →
[−1, 1] be the slope function. Let ϱ ∈ [δ 1/10 , δ µ ] and suppose that Z̃ ⊂ Z ∩ z is a rescaled (ϱ, s, ϱ−ϵ )-set
for every z = z0ϱ , z0 ∈ Z̃. Suppose that for some |r| ⩾ ϱ5/2 and for all z, z ′ ∈ Z̃ we have
(73)                                  f (z ′ ) = f (z) + r(z ′ − z) + O(rϱ1+η0 ).
Then we have κ ⩽ 2t − 2 + ζ.
Proof. Replace E by the set E ′ given by Lemma 11.1. Fix a cube q = pϱ0 for some p0 ∈ E and let Z(q)
and E(q) be the corresponding sets. It is not hard to verify that one can perform this pigeonholing step
and guarantee that the set Z̃ from the statement of the lemma satisfies |Z̃ ∩ Z(q)| ≳δO(η) |Z̃|. So we
can replace Z(q) by Z̃ ∩ Z(q) and by (b) in Lemma 11.1 keep the desired double-counting properties.
So we may assume that the approximation (73) holds for every z, z ′ ∈ Z(q). Furthermore, it still holds
that Z(q) is a rescaled (ϱ, s, ϱ−ϵ δ −Cη )-set.
  For z ∈ Z(q) we thus can write
                             Ez (q) = {(x, y + f (z)x, z), x ∈ Xy,z (q), y ∈ Yz (q)}
for rescaled (δ/ϱ, 2t − 1 − κ, δ −O(η) )-AD-regular set Yz (q) and (δ/ϱ, 1, δ −O(η) )-AD-regular set Xy,z (q).
                                                                                                          S
Let Ξq ⊂ ϱZ be the ϱ/2-covering of Ξq . For ξ ∈ Ξq let Φq,ξ ⊂ ϱZ be the ϱ/2-covering of ξ′ ∈Nϱ ξ Φq,ξ′ .
Since Θq is (ϱ, κ, δ −O(η) )-AD-regular, we have ξ∈Ξq |Φq,ξ | ∼δO(η) ϱ−κ . Let Ψ be the set of triples

(ξ, φ1 , φ2 ) such that φ1 , φ2 ∈ Φq,ξ .
    Let Z = Z(q)−z. We define a graph Gz ⊂ Yz (q)×Z ×Ψ as follows. For any tuple (p, p1 , p2 , T1 , T2 ) ∈

We include the tuple (y1 , z ′ − z, (ξ, φ1 , φ2 )) in Gz . By construction and (68), for any (y, z, (ξ, φ1 , φ2 )) ∈
Gz there is y ′ ∈ Yz (q) such that
                             y ′ = y + z(f (z + z) − f (z))(φ1 − φ2 + O(ϱ)) + O(δ).
So by linearity assumption on f ,
(74)                             y ′ = y + rz 2 (φ1 − φ2 ) + O(rϱ2+η0 + rϱ3 + δ).
Cover Yz (q) by finitely overlapping intervals Iz,j of length Crϱ2 , so that |Yz (q)∩Iz,j | ∼δO(η) (rϱ2 /δ)2t−κ−1

with α = 1 − ζ/2, β = s,γ = γ. Let χ be the constant given by Theorem 10.1. The set {rz 2 , z ∈ Z} is
clearly a rescaled (ϱ, s, Cϱ−ϵ )-set. Let Φ be the multiset of all differences φ1 − φ2 taken over all triples
(ξ, φ1 , φ2 ) ∈ Ψ. Then Φ forms a (ϱ, γ, δ −O(η) )-set (viewed as a multiset; one can turn it into a proper
set by a dyadic pigeonholing argument). So if η ≪ χµη0 then Φ is (τ, γ, τ −χ )-set and if ϵ ≪ χη0 then
{rz 2 , z ∈ Z} is a rescaled (τ, s, τ −χ )-set (restrict Z to be well separated from 0).
   From (66) and |Φ| = |Ψ| ∼δO(η) ϱ−κ−γ , we get

So for some z ∈ Z(q) we get |Gz | ⩾ δ O(η) |Z||Φ||Yz (q)|. Let Gz,j be the restriction of Gz onto the set
Yz (q) ∩ Iz,j . Then for some j we have |Gz,j | ⩾ δ O(η) |Z||Φ||Yz (q) ∩ Iz,j |. For η ≪ χµη0 we get that

and so by AD-regularity of the set Yz (q), it has rϱ2 τ -covering number at most δ −O(η) τ −(2t−κ−1) . By

giving 2t − κ − 1 ⩾ 1 − ζ/2 + O(η/µη0 ). Since η is sufficiently small, this gives us the desired bound

12.3. Small variation of slope function. In this section we show that if the function f has small
variation then this implies that tubes in T organize into planks. This will be used to contradict the
Convex Wolff axiom.
Lemma 12.5. For any ζ, µ > 0 the following holds for all sufficiently small ϵ < ϵ(ζ) and η < η(ζ, µ).
Consider a global grains (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration with s + γ ⩾ 1 and let f : Z → [−1, 1]
be the slope function. Let ϵ > 0 and ϱ ∈ [δ 1/4 , δ µ ] and suppose that Z̃ ⊂ Z ∩ z, for some z = z0 ∈ Z,
is a rescaled δ −η -uniform (ϱ, s, ϱ−ϵ )-set such that for all z, z ′ ∈ Z̃ we have |f (z) − f (z ′ )| ⩽ σ for some
σ ∈ [max(δ 1/4 , ϱ2 ), ϱ].
   Then there exists a σ × ϱ × 1 box U such that
                                                      |T[U ]|σ ⩾ ϱζ (ϱ/σ)1+γ .
Proof. Replace E by the set E ′ given by Lemma 11.1. Fix a cube q = pϱ0 for some p0 ∈ E and let Z(q)
and E(q) be the corresponding sets. It is not hard to verify that one can perform this pigeonholing
step and guarantee that the set Z̃ from the statement of the lemma satisfies |Z̃ ∩ Z(q)| ≳δO(η) |Z̃|.
Thus, by Lemma 11.1 (b) we can replace Z(q) by Z̃ ∩ Z(q) and keep the double counting properties.
Then it follows that Z(q) is a rescaled (ϱ, s, ϱ−ϵ δ −Cη )-set and |f (z) − f (z ′ )| ⩽ σ for all z, z ′ ∈ Z(q).
   For z ∈ Z(q) we thus can write
                                 Ez (q) = {(x, y + f (z)x, z), x ∈ Xy,z (q), y ∈ Yz (q)}
for rescaled (δ/ϱ, 2t − 1 − κ, δ −O(η) )-AD-regular set Yz (q) and (δ/ϱ, 1, δ −O(η) )-AD-regular set Xy,z (q).
   For T ∈ T and z ∈ Z let
                                   U (z, T ) = Nσ T + [−ϱ, ϱ] · (1, f (z), 0).
Note that U (z, T ) is approximately a σ × ϱ × 1 box.
   Consider the set of tuples Wz,z ′ ,z ′′ (q) defined for the set E(q) in Section 12.1. For a pair of tubes
 ′
T1 , T2′ and z ′′ ∈ Z(q) we write (z ′′ , T1′ ) ∼ (z ′′ , T2′ ) if they can be extended to a tuple (p, p′1 , p′2 , p′′1 , p′′2 , T1 , T2 , T1′ , T2′ ) ∈
Wz,z ′ ,z ′′ (q) for some z, z ′ ∈ Z(q).
Claim 12.6. If (z ′′ , T1′ ) ∼ (z ′′ , T2′ ) then U (z ′′ , T1′ ) ⊂ C · U (z ′′ , T2′ ).
Proof. Consider a tuple (p, p1 , p2 , p′1 , p′2 , T1 , T2 , T1′ , T2′ ) ∈ Wz,z ′ ,z ′′ (q) and write p = (x, y + f (z)x, z),
pi = (xi , yi + f (z ′ )xi , z ′ ) and p′i = (x′i , yi′ + f (z ′′ )x′i , z ′′ ). Let φ1 , φ2 , φ′1 , φ′2 denote the first coordinates
of the slopes θ(T1 ), θ(T2 ), θ(T1′ ), θ(T2′ ). Then we have by (69):
                     θ(T1′ ) − θ(T2′ ) = (φ′1 − φ′2 )(1, f (z ′ )) + (φ1 − φ2 )(0, f (z) − f (z ′ )) + O(δ)
                                           = (φ′1 − φ′2 )(1, f (z ′ )) + O(σ) = O(ϱ)(1, f (z ′′ )) + O(σ).
and by (71), (72):
                                      x′1 − x′2 = (z ′ − z)(φ1 − φ2 ) + O(ϱ2 ) = O(ϱ),
                                       y1′ − y2′ = O(ϱσ + δ) = O(ϱσ).
From this we conclude that p′2 ∈ U (z ′′ , T1′ ) and T2′ ⊂ C ·U (z ′′ , T1′ ) which implies U (z ′′ , T1′ ) and U (z ′′ , T2′ )
define essentially the same box, concluding the proof.                                                                     □

  Let Ξq ⊂ ϱZ be the set whose ϱ/2 neighborhood covers Ξq and let Φq,ξ ⊂ ϱZ be the set whose ϱ/2

                                            1      X

where we note that the number of valid pairs (z ′′ , T1′ ) is approximately |E(q)|δ −κ . So it follows
that on average the graph G(z ′′ , T1′ ) is δ O(η) -dense. Let P ′ ⊂ P be the subset of all (z ′′ , T1′ ) with
|G(z ′′ , T1′ )| ⩾ δ C1 η |Z(q)|2 ϱ−2γ for some large constant C1 . It follows from the above that most of the
W -tuples are captured by pairs (z, T ) ∼ (z, T ′ ) with both (z, T ), (z, T ′ ) ∈ P ′ . Let G ′ ⊂ P ′ × P ′ be the
adjacency graph for the relation (z, T ) ∼ (z, T ′ ).
   Fix some (z, T ) ∈ P ′ and for an integer m ⩾ 1 consider the ℓ-th neighborhood set Nm (z, T ) ⊂ P ′ in
G, i.e. this is the set of (z, T ′ ) ∈ P ′ which are connected to (z, T ) by a path with at most m edges (note
that the z-coordinate doesn’t change along the edges of G ′ ). By Claim 12.6 all pairs (z, T ′ ) ∈ Nℓ (z, T )
are all contained in the same convex set Cm · U (z, T ). So it suffices to show that for a bounded m,
the neighborhood is large.
   Now we observe that for (z ′′ , T1′ ) ∼ (z ′′ , T2′ ), if we denote by p′1 = (x′1 , y1′ + f (z ′′ )x′1 , z ′′ ) and p′2 =
(x′2 , y2′ + f (z ′′ )x′2 , z ′′ ) the points in T1′ ∩ Ez ′′ and T2′ ∩ Ez ′′ , respectively, then by (71) we get
                                         x′1 − x′2 = (z ′ − z)(φ1 − φ2 ) + O(ϱ2 )
holds. For (z, T ′ ) ∈ P ′ let x(z, T ′ ) be the x-coordinate of the intersection point T ′ ∩ Ez (q). Now let
(z, T ′ ) ∈ Nm (z, T ) be arbitrary and let (z1 , z2 , φ1 , φ2 ) ∈ G(z, T ′ ). Then it follows from the above that
there exists some (z, T ′′ ) ∈ Nm+1 (z, T ) such that
                                x(z, T ′′ ) − x(z, T ′ ) = (z2 − z1 )(φ2 − φ1 ) + O(ϱ2 ).
Let Xm be a maximal ϱ2 -separated subset of points x(z, T ′ ) over all (z, T ′ ) ∈ Nm (z, T ). We obtain a
graph
                             Gm ⊂ Xm × Z(q) × Z(q) × Φq,ξ × Φq,ξ
where ξ ∈ Ξq is such that θ(T ) ∈ Nϱ (φ, ξ + f (z)φ), so that for every (x, z1 , z2 , φ1 , φ2 ) ∈ Gm there
exists x′ ∈ Xm+1 such that
                               x′ − x = (z2 − z1 )(φ2 − φ1 ) + O(ϱ2 ).
Furthermore, we have the lower bound
                                              |Gm | ⩾ δ O(η) |Xm ||Z(q)|2 ϱ−2γ .
Recall that Z(q) is rescaled (ϱ, s, δ −O(η) ϱ−ϵ )-set and Φq,ξ is (ϱ, γ, δ −O(η) )-AD-regular. Using the as-
sumption s + γ ⩾ 1 and Theorem 10.1, we either have |Xm | ⩾ ϱ−1+ζ or we get |Xm+1 | ⩾ ϱ−χ |Xm |.
Here ζ > 0 is from the statement of the lemma and χ is given by Theorem 10.1 and it is a function of
α = 1 − ζ, β = s, γ = γ.
   By the pigeonhole principle, we can find some m = Oχ (1) so that |Xm+1 | < ϱ−χ |Xm | holds. So
for this choice of m it must be the case that |Xm | ⩾ ϱζ−1 . Let {x1 , . . . , xN } ⊂ Xm be a maximal
σ-separated subset (recall σ ⩾ ϱ2 ). For each xi we have a corresponding pair (z, Ti ) ∈ Nℓ (z, T ) and
a corresponding point pi ∈ Ez ∩ Ti . We know that Ti ⊂ Cm · U (z, T ). Furthermore, if we write
θ(Ti ) ∈ Nδ (φi , ξi + f (z)φi ) for some ξi ∈ Ξpi and φi ∈ Φpi ,ξi , then using AD-regularity of these sets,
                        KAKEYA AND FURSTENBERG PROBLEMS FOR STICKY SETS OF TUBES                                                        49


we get ∼δO(η) (σ/ϱ)−γ -many σ-tubes T̃i ∈ Tσ which intersect the z-plane within σ-ball around Ti ∩ Ez
and satisfy θ(T̃i ) = (φ̃i , ξi + f (z)φ̃i + O(σ)) with some φ̃i = φi + O(ϱ). Indeed, we choose some
φ′i ∈ Φpi ,ξi within distance ϱ from φi , then we choose a tube Ti′ ∈ Tpi in direction (φ′i , ξi + f (z)φ′i ) and
let T̃i = (Ti′ )σ . For large enough constant K = Oχ,η (1) we get
                                |Tσ [K · U (z, T )]| ≳δO(η) N (ϱ/σ)γ ≳δO(η) ϱζ (ϱ/σ)(ϱ/σ)γ .
This finishes the proof after replacing ζ with ζ/2 and taking η ≪ µζ.                                                                   □

                                               13. Non-linear slope function
  The goal of this section is to deal with the most difficult case when the slope function f is approxi-

Additive relations. Fix an L-tuple and write, for i = 1, 2,

In the applications, |f (z ′ ) − f (z ′′ )| ⩽ ϱ and δ ⩽ τ ϱ, so the error in (77) is O(τ ϱ).

13.2. Kaufman and radial projections in R2 . We will need a variant of the radial projection
theorem proved by Orponen, Shmerkin and Wang [22]. For points p, p′ ∈ R2 let us denote Tp,p′ (ϱ) the
ϱ × 1-tube around the line pp′ .
Theorem 13.2. Let t ∈ (0, 2] and ε1 , ε2 , ζ ∈ (0, 1). Suppose that ε1 ⩽ ζ/8 .Then there exists δ0 =
δ0 (t, ε1 , ε2 , ζ), η0 = η0 (t, ε1 , ζ) > 0 such that the following holds for all δ < δ0 , η < η0 . Let P ⊂ [0, 1]2
be a δ-separated (δ, δ −η )-uniform (δ, t, δ −η )-set such that |P ∩ T | ⩽ δ ε2 |P | for any δ ε1 × 1-tube T .
    Then there exists a subset G ⊂ P × P such that |G| ⩾ (1 − δ min(η,ε2 /2) )|P |2 such that for every
(p, p′ ) ∈ G we have |Tp,p′ (ϱ) ∩ P | ⩽ δ −ζ ϱmin(1,t) |P | for all ϱ ∈ [δ, 1].
    In particular, for every p ∈ P such that |G|p | ⩾ ν|P |, where G|p := {p′ ∈ P : (p, p′ ) ∈ G}, the set of
tubes {Tp,p′ (δ), (p, p′ ) ∈ G} is a (δ, min(1, t), Cδ −ζ ν −1 )-set.
  This version is different from the discretized radial projection theorem stated in [22, Corollary 2.22]
and does not appear to follow directly from it. We give a proof of Theorem 13.2 in Appendix A.
Remark 13.3. Note that in the statement of Theorem 13.2 the Frostman constant δ −η does not depend
on the two-ends parameter ε2 . This is similar to how the quantitative bounds in [22] or [29] do not
depend on the ‘2-ends exponents’. This feature is used in an important way in the proof of Lemma 22.5
(originally we also used it in the proof of Theorem 1.1 but managed to simplify the argument later).
  We will also need Kaufman’s projection theorem, we will use a slightly stronger version compared to
the one stated in [32, Theorem 8.2]. For λ ∈ [−1, 1] we denote πλ (x, y) = x − λy the linear projection
with slope λ.
Theorem 13.4. Let t ∈ (0, 1] and let ε > 0. The following holds for δ ⩽ δ0 and η < η0 .
   Let P ⊂ [−1, 1]2 be a δ-separated (δ, t, δ −η )-set and let Λ ⊂ [−1, 1] be a δ-separated (δ, t, δ −η )-set.
Then there exists Λ′ ⊂ Λ, |Λ′ | ⩾ (1 − δ η )|Λ|, and Pλ ⊂ P for each λ ∈ Λ′ , |Pλ | ⩾ (1 − δ η )|P | such that
for any Pλ′ ⊂ Pλ with |Pλ′ | ⩾ δ η |Pλ |, πλ (Pλ′ ) is a (δ, t, δ −ε )-set (if there are overlap, view projection as
multiset).
52                                            HONG WANG, DMITRII ZAKHAROV

13.3. Non-linearly rotating grains. In this section we consider the case when f is not approximated
by a linear function and has maximum variation. The main tool in the following ABC sum-product-
type statement.
Theorem 13.5. Let α, β ∈ (0, 1], γ ⩾ 0 such that min(β + γ, 1) > α and let ε1 , ε2 > 0 such that
ε1 ≪α,β,γ 1. Then there exist η0 = η0 (α, β, γ, ε1 ), η 0 = η0 (α, β, γ, ε1 , ε2 ) such that the following holds
for all η < η0 , η < η 0 and small enough δ. Let B ⊂ [−1, 1] be a δ-separated (δ, β, δ −η )-set. Let
C ⊂ [1, 2] be a nonempty δ-separated (δ, γ, δ −η )-set (when γ = 0, C could be a single point). Let
F : B → [−1, 1] be a function such that for any r, u ∈ R:
(78)                                  #{b ∈ B : |F (b) − rb − u| ⩽ δ ε1 } ⩽ δ ε2 |B|.
Let A ⊂ [−1, 1] be a δ-separated subset. Let G ⊂ A × B × B × C be a subset of density at least δ η and
suppose that
(79)                        |{a + c(b − b′ )(F (b) − F (b′ )), (a, b, b′ , c) ∈ G}|δ ⩽ δ −η |A|.
Then we have |A| ⩾ δ −α .
  In particular, if β > α, then suppose G ⊂ A × B × B has density at least δ η and
                                |{a + (b − b′ )(F (b) − F (b′ )), (a, b, b′ ) ∈ G}|δ ⩽ δ −η |A|.
Then we have |A| ⩾ δ −α .
                     KAKEYA AND FURSTENBERG PROBLEMS FOR STICKY SETS OF TUBES                                       53

  In other words, if a set A is essentially closed under translations by elements c(b − b′ )(F (b) − F (b′ )),
then A must have ‘dimension’ at least min(β + γ, 1). This is similar to the ABC sum-product theorem
except for the expression (b − b′ )(F (b) − F (b′ )) appearing in the statement. In the application, we will
take η = η, so the reader can restrict to this case. In general, having η not depend on ε2 provides some
extra flexibility but it will not be needed here.
Remark 13.6. The analogous statement over C is false: take F (b) = b (complex conjugation) and
take A = [−1, 1] ⊂ C, C = [1, 2] and B = [−1, 1] + i[−1, 1] ⊂ C. For this choice we have dimC A =
dimC C = γ = 1/2, dimC B = β = 1 and A is essentially invariant under translation by elements
c(b − b′ )(F (b) − F (b′ )) = c|b − b′ |2 ∈ R.

Lemma 13.9. Let ζ, µ, ε1 , ε2 > 0 be so that ε1 is sufficiently small in ζ. Then the following holds for
all η < η(ζ, µ, ε1 , ε2 ) and ϵ < ϵ(ζ, ε1 ).

(ϱ, s, ϱ−ϵ )-set for some z0 ∈ Z. Suppose that there is σ ∈ [ϱ2 , ϱ] such that for all z, z ′ ∈ Z̃ we have

(94)                               #{z ∈ Z̃ : |f (z) − rz − u| ⩽ ϱε1 σ} ⩽ ϱε2 |Z̃|.

Then we have κ ⩽ 2t − 2 + ζ.
Proof. Replace E by the set E ′ given by Lemma 11.1. Fix a cube q = pϱ0 for some p0 ∈ E and let Z(q)

Thus, by Lemma 11.1 (b) we can replace Z(q) by Z̃ ∩ Z(q) and keep the double counting properties.
Then it follows that Z(q) is a rescaled (ϱ, s, ϱ−ϵ δ −Cη )-set and (94) is satisfied with Z(q) in place of Z̃
and with δ −Cη ϱε2 in place of ϱε2 .
   For z ∈ Z(q) we thus can write
                                    Ez (q) = {(x, y + f (z)x, z), x ∈ Xy,z (q), y ∈ Yz (q)}
for rescaled (δ/ϱ, 2t − 1 − κ, δ −O(η) )-AD-regular set Yz (q) and (δ/ϱ, 1, δ −O(η) )-AD-regular set Xy,z (q).
Let Ξq ⊂ ϱZ be the set who ϱ/2-neighborhood covers Ξq . For ξ ∈ Ξq let Φq,ξ ⊂ ϱZ be the set whose
ϱ/2-neighborhood covers ξ′ ∈Nϱ/2 ξ Φq,ξ′ . By AD-regularity of Θq we have ξ∈Ξq |Φq,ξ | ∼δO(η) ϱ−κ .

                 14. Putting everything together: Proof of Proposition 6.5
   Suppose that for any δ0 , η0 there are δ < δ0 and η < η0 for which there exists a (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-
Furstenberg configuration. Here we assume that γ ⩾ max{s + κ − 1 − ϵ0 , 1 − s + ϵ0 }. Our goal is to
show that κ ⩽ max(2t − 2, t − s) + 4ϵ0 holds, for the sake of contradiction suppose the contrary.
   By Propositions 11.2 and 11.3 we can construct a new (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration
(perhaps for different but also arbitrarily small δ, η) for which the sets Ez and Θp have the global
grains structure given by some slope function f : Z → [−1, 1]. In other words, we obtain a global
grains (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration (T, E, {Tp }) where T is a (2t, δ −η )-AD-regular set of
tubes satisfying Ct−CW (T) ⩽ δ −η .
   Now we split into three cases depending on the behavior of the function f and reduce to either
Lemma 12.5, Lemma 12.4 or Lemma 13.9. Recall that by Definition 11.2, the pair (Z, f ) is δ η -uniform.
Fix an arbitrary z0 ∈ Z. Let ϵ > 0 and let ϱ ∈ [δ 1/10 , δ µ ] be a scale such that Z ∩ z, z = z0ϱ , is a rescaled
(ϱ, s, ϱ−ϵ )-set (such ϱ exists by Lemma 7.3). Apply Lemma 11.1 and replace E by the set E ′ and Z by
Z ′ = {z : Ez′ ̸= ∅}. For every p0 ∈ Ez0 and q = pϱ0 we then have well-defined sets Z(q) ⊂ Z ∩ z, z = z0ϱ
and E(q).
   Let 0 < ζ < ϵ0 /2 and suppose that κ ⩾ t − s + 2ϵ0 . Then by Lemma 11.4, we can refine Z(q) to a
subset of density δ O(η/ζ) such that |f (z) − f (z ′ )| ⩽ ϱ for every z, z ′ ∈ Z(q). After further pigeonholing
we may assume the same uniformity conditions hold for the new Z(q).
   Next, we show that f cannot vary much slower than an ≈ 1-Lischitz function.
Claim 14.1. For any 0 < ε1 ⩽ ϵ0 /2 and ϵ > 0 there exists ε2 > 0 such that the following holds.
Suppose that for every choice of q as above and z0 ∈ Z(q),

                     15. Fully non-coplanar case: proof of Proposition 6.6
   In this section we consider the case when γ ⩽ ϵ0 and s + κ ⩽ 1 + ϵ0 . The proof in Section 10 does
not apply because the direction sets Φξ might be too small to apply the ABC sum-product theorem.
In this section we present a slightly different argument that fixes this issue. We will use Ren–Wang’s
Furstenberg set estimate [25] in the following form.

Remark 15.2. In this argument, we could have used the radial projections theorem to prove that Ta
are κ-dimensional as long as γ < κ − ϵ0 . Since this range is already covered by Proposition 6.4, we
don’t need to do this. Note that the argument in Section 10 is still necessary as it covers the case
s + κ > 1 while the bound in this section can be lossy there.

                       16. Fully coplanar case: proof of Proposition 6.7
   In this section we consider the coplanar case γ ⩾ κ − ϵ1 and s + κ < 1 + ϵ0 . Here we can choose ϵ1
arbitrarily small compared to ϵ0 and our goal is to prove κ ⩽ κ0 (s, t) + 2ϵ0 . Note that the coplanar
reduction argument from Section 11 requires s + γ ⩾ 1 in order to guarantee that the sets Xy,z
parameterizing global grains are 1-dimensional. First, we show that after rescaling, we can obtain a
global grains structure where the grains are filled in by at least s + κ-dimensional sets. This will allow
us to run a modified version of the arguments in Sections 12–14. Similarly to those sections we will
consider the non-linear, linear and slow variation cases. The proofs follow quite closely the arguments
from the above sections, so we omit various technical details and focus on the required modifications.
Proposition 16.1. Fix s, t, κ such that s + κ ⩽ 1 + ϵ0 and κ ⩾ max( 32 (t − s), 0) + 2ϵ0 . Suppose that
for every ϵ1 > 0 and η0 , δ0 there are δ ⩽ δ0 , η ⩽ η0 such that there is a (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg
configuration with γ ⩾ κ − ϵ1 and whose set of tubes has t-Convex Wolff constant at most δ −η .
   Then for arbitrarily small η, δ one can construct a (δ, η, s, t, κ, κ)-Furstenberg configuration (T, E, {Tp })
with the following properties.
                    KAKEYA AND FURSTENBERG PROBLEMS FOR STICKY SETS OF TUBES                                    61

    (i) We can write
                                   Az = {(x, y + f (z)x), x ∈ Xy,z , y ∈ Yz },
        for some f : Z → [−1, 1]. Here Yz is (δ, b, δ −η )-AD-regular and Xy,z is (δ, a, δ −η )-AD-regular
        with
                                    a + b = 2t − κ, a ⩾ s + κ.
        Furthermore, for every ϱ × τ tube T , we have |Az ∩ T |ϱ ≲ δ −η (τ /ϱ)a .
   (ii) For every p ∈ Ez , there is a (δ, κ, δ −η )-AD-regular set Φp ⊂ [−1, 1] such that for every φ ∈ Φp
        there is T ∈ T containing p such that θ(T ) ∈ Nδ (φ, ξp + f (z)φ). We denote by Tp the set of all
        such tubes.
           The function f : Z → [−1, 1] is dyadic Lipschitz, i.e. for every z = z ϱ and z1 , z2 ∈ z ∩ Z we
        have |f (z) − f (z ′ )| ≲ ϱ.
  (iii) The set T is t-Convex Wolff, i.e. Ct−CW (T) ⩽ δ −η .
Proof. Fix η0 > 0, let ϵ0 ≪ η0 and fix δ0 . Consider an (δ, η, s, t, κ, γ)-Furstenberg configuration such
that γ ⩾ κ − ϵ1 with η, δ chosen sufficiently small with respect to other parameters.
    By applying Lemma 5.3 to sets Az and passing to a refinement, we can find a pair of scales ϱ, τ ∈
[δ, 1] such that the sets Az ∩ Bϱ rescale to nearly (ϱ/τ, 2t − κ, a, (ϱ/τ )−ϱ )-aligned sets. Here we fix
some ζ ≪ η0 and we have ϱ/τ ⩽ δ χ(ζ) and a ∈ [0, 2t − κ]. Now we can apply the rescaling and
thickening map T 7→ TTϱ τ for a fixed Tτ ∈ Tτ and define the corresponding Furstenberg configuration
on scale δ̃ = ϱ/τ . By adjusting the new set Ẽ below scale δ̃, we can ensure that the new sets Ã are
(δ̃, 2t − κ, a, d˜−ζ δ −Cη )-aligned. It follows that we can write Ãz = {(x, y + f (z)x, z), x ∈ X̃y,z , y ∈ Ỹz }
where X̃y,z is (δ̃, a, δ̃ −O(ζ) )-AD-regular, Ỹz is (δ̃, b, δ̃ −O(ζ) )-AD-regular (b = 2t − κ − a) and Ãz is tube
Katz-Tao. We can check that a ⩾ s + κ holds by running the same argument as in Proposition 11.2.
When we apply the ABC sum-product theorem, we use the fact that min(s + γ, 1) ⩾ s + κ − ϵ0 to
conclude that a ⩾ s + κ − ϵ0 − ϵ. By adjusting the error terms we can turn this into a ⩾ s + κ, as
desired. This gives (i) with η0 in place of η if we choose parameters sufficiently small.
    To establish (ii), we can run the proof of Proposition 11.3 again: if the slope αq describing the sets
Θp , p ∈ E(q), differs significantly from f (z), then we can estimate the ϱ2 -covering number of Az (q) by
considering orthogonal projections along vectors (1, f (z)) and (1, αq ). These projections have at least
s + κ dimensional fibers, so this leads to the inequality
                                       2t − κ ⩽ 2(2t − κ − (s + κ)) + o(1),
so κ ⩽ 32 (t − s) + o(1) < 23 (t − s) + 2ϵ0 for small enough parameters, as desired. The dyadic Lipschitz
property of f is established in the same way (see e.g. proof of Lemma 11.4).                                    □

Remark 16.4. In this last step, it suffices to use a weaker Convex–Wolff axiom. Indeed, suppose that
Ct;t′ −CW (T) ⩽ δ −η for some parameter t′ (see Definition 3.10). Then to make the above argument
work, we need to require t′ < s + 2κ. Recalling that κ0 (s, t) = max(0, t − s, 2t − 2) and in this section
s + κ ⩽ 1, this leads to the following permissible t′ :
                                          (
                                      ′    min(2t, s), t ∈ (0, s],
                                     t =
                                           2t − s, t ∈ [s, 1].
Note that if t > 1 then κ ⩾ t − s ⩾ 1 − s, so this contradicts the assumption s + κ ⩽ 1. Thus,
for Proposition 6.7, i.e. the case when s + κ ⩽ 1 and γ ⩾ κ − ϵ0 it suffices to assume this weaker
(t; t′ )-Convex Wolff axiom.

                                     Part 2. Sticky Kakeya
                              17. Setup for Sticky Kakeya
                                             P                 P
  We say that a shading Y on T is λ-dense, if T ∈T |Y (T )| ⩾ λ T ∈T |T | (where | · | denotes the
volume).
Definition 17.1. Let Kd (κ) denote the following statement.
  For any ϵ > 0 there exists η = η(d, ϵ) > 0 such that the following holds for δ > 0 sufficiently small.
Let T be a (d − 1, δ −η )-AD-regular set of δ-tubes in Rd and CCW (T) ⩽ δ −η . Suppose Y is a δ η -dense
shading on T. Then
                                          |U (T, Y )|δ ⩾ δ ϵ δ −d+κ .
  Let Mat(k, ℓ) denote the set of k × ℓ matrices with entries in [−1, 1].
Definition 17.2. Let d ⩾ 3 and ℓ ∈ {2, . . . , d−1}. A global (ℓ−1)-grains (δ, η, κ)-Kakeya configuration
(E, T, {Tp }p∈E ) in Rd is the following collection of data:
   (1) A (d − 1, δ −η )-AD-regular set of δ-tubes T.
   (2) A δ-separated set Z ⊂ [−1, 1] with |Z| ⩾ δ η−1 and a f : Z → Mat(d − ℓ, ℓ − 1) satisfying
        “dyadic Lipchitz continuity”: for any ϱ ∈ [δ, 1] ∩ δ ηZ and any z1 , z2 in the same dyadic ϱ-
        interval, |f (z1 ) − f (z2 )| ≲ ϱ, where | · | means ℓ∞ -norm over all entries of the matrix.
   (3) A δ-separated set E ⊂ [−1, 1]d of the form
                               G
                        E=         Ez = {(x, y + f (z)x, z), z ∈ Z, y ∈ Yz , x ∈ Xy,z },
                            z∈Z

       where Yz ⊂ [−1, 1]d−ℓ is (δ, d − ℓ − κ, δ −η )-AD-regular set and Xy,z ⊂ [−1, 1]ℓ−1 is such that
                   η−ℓ+1 . In addition, for every ϱ ∈ [δ, 1] ∩ δ ηZ and a dyadic ϱ-interval z such that
       |Xy,z |δ ⩾ δS
       Z ∩ z ̸= ∅, z∈z∩Z Yz is a (ϱ, d − ℓ − κ, δ −η )-AD-regular set.
   (4) For each p ∈ Ez there is ξp ∈ [−1, 1]d−ℓ and (δ, κ, δ −η )-AD-regular set Φp ⊂ [−1, 1]ℓ−1 such that
       for every φ ∈ Φp there is a tube T ∈ T through p such that θ(T ) = (φ, ξp + f (z)φ) + O(δ). In
       addition, for any ϱ ∈ [δ, 1]∩δ ηZ , write q = pϱ , then there is ξq ∈ [−1, 1]d−ℓ and a (ϱ, κ, δ −η )-AD
       regular set Φq ⊂ [−1, 1]ℓ−1 such that Φp′ ⊂ Φϱq and |ξp′ − ξq | ≲ ϱ for any p′ ∈ E ∩ q (including
       p).
Remark 17.1. Given a global (ℓ−1)-grains (δ, η, κ)-Kakeya configuration, we can also define a shading
on the corresponding set of δ-tubes T: for each T ∈ T, let Y (T ) be the union of dyadic δ-cubes p(δ)
over p ∈ Ez , z ∈ Z such that θ(T ) = (φ, ξp + f (z)φ) + O(δ). It follows from Definition 17.2 that this
shading is ⪆ δ Cη -dense.
Definition 17.3 (Slab Frostman, Tube Katz-Tao). We say that a set of points P ⊂ [−1, 1]d is (δ, s, C)-
slab Frostman if for any ϱ ∈ [δ, 1] and any ϱ × 1 × . . . × 1 slab S ⊂ Rd we have |P ∩ S| ⩽ Cϱs |P |.
Similarly, let us say that P is (δ, s, C)-tube Katz-Tao if for any ϱ ∈ [δ, 1], τ ∈ [ϱ, 1] and any ϱ×· · ·×ϱ×τ
tube T ⊂ Rd we have |P ∩ T |ϱ ⩽ C(τ /ϱ)s .
   If, in addition, the set Φp is (δ, γ, δ −η )-slab Frostman for every p ∈ E, then we say that (E, T, {Tp })
is an ℓ-linear (δ, η, κ, γ)-Kakeya configuration. If for every z ∈ Z the set Yz is (δ, 1 − γ, δ −η )-tube Katz
Tao, then we say that E is an ℓ-planar (δ, η, κ, γ)-Kakeya configuration. If both of these conditions are
satisfied then we call E an ℓ-planar ℓ-linear (δ, η, κ, γ)-Kakeya configuration.
   These conditions ensure that the slope function f gives a non-trivial description of the Kakeya
configuration E. Indeed, if the Kakeya configuration is not ℓ-linear, then the set of tubes though a
point Tp spans some ℓ′ -dimensional plane for ℓ′ < ℓ. In this case, we can replace the ℓ-dimensional
global grains by ℓ′ -dimensional grains and study the resulting ℓ′ -linear Kakeya configuration. If the
Kakeya configuration is not ℓ-planar for some tiny γ > 0, then this means that sets Yz concentrate a lot
in tubes. Since Yz is AD-regular, the structure theorem for AD-regular sets (Proposition 5.3) implies
66                                         HONG WANG, DMITRII ZAKHAROV

that after some rescaling Yz is a union of essentially fully filled out parallel tubes. So we can replace
global (ℓ − 1)-grains by ℓ-dimensional grains and study the resulting global ℓ-grains configuration.
   In fact, we will need a more general definition of a k-planar ℓ-linear Kakeya configuration for an
arbitrary pair k ⩾ ℓ ∈ {2, . . . , d − 1}. In this definition, E has (k − 1)-dimensional horizontal global
grains that are parameterized by a slope function f˜ and the set of tubes passing through a fixed point
p ∈ E is contained in an ℓ-dimensional plane parameterized by a slope function f .
Definition 17.4. Let d ⩾ 3 and ℓ ⩽ k ∈ {2, . . . , d − 1}. A ℓ-linear k-planar (δ, η, κ, γ)-Kakeya
configuration (E, T, {Tp }p∈E ) in Rd is a global (ℓ−1)-grains (δ, η, κ)-Kakeya configuration with Z, f, E,
Φp for each p ∈ E, and the following additional information:
   (1) For Z ⊂ [−1, 1] and f : Z → Mat(d−ℓ, ℓ−1) there is a slope function f˜ : Z → Mat(d−k, k −1)
        such that if we further write
                                               
                                                f1
                                         f=         , f˜ = (f˜1 , f˜2 )
                                                f2

          where f1 ∈ Mat(k − ℓ, ℓ − 1), f2 , f˜1 ∈ Mat(d − k, ℓ − 1) and f˜2 ∈ Mat(d − k, k − ℓ), they obey
          the following consistency relation:
(103)                                        f2 (z) = f˜1 (z) + f˜2 (z)f1 (z).
      (2) The δ-separated set E ⊂ [−1, 1]d can be written as
                             G
                       E=       Ez = {(x̃, ỹ + f˜(z)x̃, z), z ∈ Z, ỹ ∈ Ỹz , x̃ ∈ X̃y,z },
                                z∈Z

          where Ỹz ⊂ [−1, 1]d−k is (δ, d − k − κ, δ −η )-AD-regular set2 and X̃y,z ⊂ [−1, 1]k−1 is such that
          |Xy,z |δ ⩾ δ η−k+1 . Here we view x̃, ỹ as column vectors.
      (3) Φp ⊂ [−1, 1]ℓ−1 is (δ, γ, δ −η )-slab Frostman for every p ∈ E.
      (4) Yz ⊂ [−1, 1]d−k is (δ, 1 − γ, δ −η )-tube Katz-Tao for every z ∈ Z.
   Note that equation (103) reflects the fact that the set of directions of tubes in Tp is contained in a
translate of the k-dimensional plane at slope f˜.
   If ℓ = 2 then the set Φp is automatically (δ, κ, δ −η )-slab Frostman. Similarly, if k = d − 1, then the
set Yz is automatically (δ, 1 − κ, δ −η )-tube Katz-Tao. In some situations we only need to keep track of
only one of the two slope functions f˜ or f in which case the simpler Definition 17.2 would suffice.
   The proof of Theorem 1.2 splits into the following steps. Some of the steps can be generalized to
the higher dimensional sticky Kakeya and so we state those for arbitrary dimension d.
   First, we show that if κ is the smallest value such that Kd (κ) is true then we can construct a k-planar
ℓ-linear (δ, η, κ, γ)-Kakeya configuration for some ℓ ⩽ k ∈ {2, . . . , d − 1}.
Proposition 17.2. Let κ be the infimum of the set of κ′ such that Kd (κ′ ) holds and assume that
κ > 0. Let g : (0, 1) → (0, 1), h : (0, 1)2 → (0, 1) be continuous functions. Then for any δ0 , η0 there
exist δ ∈ (0, δ0 ), η ∈ (0, η0 ), γ > 0 and ℓ ⩽ k ∈ {2, . . . , d − 1} so that there exists a k-planar ℓ-linear
(δ, η, κ, γ)-Kakeya configuration whose set T of δ-tubes satisfies CCW (T) ⩽ δ −η and we have η < g(γ)
and δ ⩽ h(η, γ).
   A typical choice of the decreasing functions would be h(η, γ) = exp(−η −2 ) and g(γ) = γ 2 .
   This proposition reduces the sticky Kakeya problem in R4 to analyzing Kakeya configurations with
(k, ℓ) = (3, 3), (2, 2) and (3, 2). We start by dealing with the case of (d − 1)-linear configurations in
Rd .
     2We sometimes abuse the notation and reuse Y for Ỹ to denote a different object than the one in Definition 17.2. It
                                                 z      z
should be clear from context which one we mean and they rarely show up the same time.
                    KAKEYA AND FURSTENBERG PROBLEMS FOR STICKY SETS OF TUBES                                   67

Proposition 17.3. Let ζ > 0, γ > 0 and d ⩾ 3. The following holds for η ⩽ η17.3 (ζ, γ, d) and
δ ⩽ δ0 (η, γ, ζ, d). Suppose that there exists a (d − 1)-linear (δ, η, κ, γ)-Kakeya configuration in Rd
whose set of tubes T satisfies CCW (T) ⩽ δ −η . Then we have κ ⩽ ζ.
  Next, we consider 2-planar Kakeya configurations in Rd .
Proposition 17.4. Let ζ > 0 and d ⩾ 3. The following holds for γ > 0, η ⩽ η17.4 (ζ, γ, d) and
δ ⩽ δ0 (η, γ, ζ, d). Suppose that there exists a 2-planar (δ, η, κ, γ)-Kakeya configuration in Rd whose set
of tubes T satisfies CCW (T) ⩽ δ −η . Then we have κ ⩽ ζ.
  Note that either of Propositions 17.3 or 17.4 suffices to prove the sticky Kakeya conjecture in R3 .
The proofs of these propositions generalize two different arguments outlines in Section 2. In R4 , it
remains to consider 3-planar 2-linear Kakeya configurations.
Proposition 17.5. Let ζ > 0. The following holds for γ > 0, η ⩽ η17.5 (ζ, γ) and δ ⩽ δ0 (η, γ, ζ).
Suppose that there exists a 3-planar 2-linear (δ, η, κ, γ)-Kakeya configuration in R4 whose set of tubes
T satisfies CCW (T) ⩽ δ −η . Then we have κ ⩽ ζ.
  By combining these propositions we can deduce Theorem 1.2.

Proof of Theorem 1.2. Note that Theorem 1.2 is equivalent to the statement K4 (0). Let κ be the
infimum of all κ′ such that K4 (κ′ ) holds. For the sake of contradiction suppose that κ > 0. Let ζ = κ/2
and let g(γ) = min{η17.3 (ζ, γ, 4), η17.4 (ζ, γ, 4), η17.5 (ζ, γ)} (without loss of generality the η0 -functions
from these propositions are monotone decreasing). Similarly, let us define h(η) as the minimum of
functions δ0 (η, γ, ζ, d) appearing in the statements of propositions. By Proposition 17.2, there is a
pair (k, ℓ) ∈ {(2, 2), (3, 2), (3, 3)} such that for any δ0 , η0 there exist γ > 0, δ < δ0 , η < η0 and a k-
planar ℓ-linear (δ, η, κ, γ)-Kakeya configuration such that η < g(γ) and the set of δ-tubes T satisfies
CCW (T) ⩽ δ −η and δ is can be taken arbitrarily small compared to η, γ. Then for (k, ℓ) = (3, 3) by
Proposition 17.3, for (k, ℓ) = (2, 2) by Proposition 17.4 and for (k, ℓ) = (3, 2) Proposition 17.5 we
conclude that κ ⩽ ζ.                                                                                          □

         18. Construction of Kakeya configurations: proof of Proposition 17.2
  Let us restate Proposition 17.2 for convenience.
Proposition 18.1. Let κ be the infimum of the set of κ′ such that Kd (κ′ ) holds. Let g : (0, 1) → (0, 1),
h : (0, 1)2 → (0, 1) be monotone decreasing functions. Then for any δ0 , η0 there exist δ ∈ (0, δ0 ),
η ∈ (0, η0 ), γ > 0 and ℓ ⩽ k ∈ {2, . . . , d − 1} so that there exists a k-planar ℓ-linear (δ, η, κ, γ)-Kakeya
configuration whose set T of δ-tubes satisfies CCW (T) ⩽ δ −η and we have η < g(γ) and δ ⩽ h(η, γ).
  We first prove a weaker version and then upgrade.
Proposition 18.2. Let κ be the infimum of the set of κ′ such that Kd (κ′ ) holds. Then for any δ0 , η0
there exists δ < δ0 , η ⩽ η0 , and ℓ ∈ {2, . . . , d − 1} so that there exists a global (ℓ − 1)-grains (δ, η, κ)-
Kakeya configuration whose set T of δ-tubes satisfies CCW (T) ⩽ δ −η .
Proof. Since κ is the infimum of all κ′ such that Kd (κ′ ) holds. Then for any ν > 0, Kd (κ − ν) does not
hold. Therefore there exists ϵ > 0 such that for all sufficiently small η > 0 and δ > 0, there exists a
(d − 1, δ −η )-AD-regular set of δ-tubes with CCW (T) ⩽ δ −η and a δ η -dense shading Y on T and
                                    |U (T, Y )|δ ⩽ δ ϵ δ −d+κ−ν ⩽ δ −d+κ−ν ,
Since the properties of Kakeya configurations are monotone in η, we may replace the parameter η by
max(η, ν), i.e. without loss of generality,
(104)                                      |U (T, Y )|δ ⩽ δ −η δ −d+κ .
68                                     HONG WANG, DMITRII ZAKHAROV

Equivalently,

(105)                           µ(T, Y ) :=                    ⩾ δ −κ+η ⩾ δ −κ+η .

    For any η0 > 0, let η ≪ ϵ1 ≪ ηd ≪ ηd−1 ≪ · · · ≪ η1 ≪ η0 be small constants to be determined. Let
δ η0 ≪ ϱd−1 ≪ · · · ≪ ϱ1 ≪ δ0 be small scales to be determined. We are going to show that for any δ0
and η0 , there exists δ̃ ⩽ δ0 , η̃ < η0 , and ℓ ∈ {2, . . . , d − 1} so that there exists a global (ℓ − 1)-grains
(δ̃, η̃, κ)-Kakeya configuration in Rd . We are going to take η, δ sufficiently small compared to η0 , δ0 ,
respectively.
Step 1. Pigeonhole ℓ-linear points. After pigeonholing and passing to a ⪆ 1-refinement of Y , we may
assume that TY (p) is ≈ δ −η -uniform for each p ∈ U (T, Y ): |TY (p)[T ϱ ]| is approximately the same for
each T ∈ TY (p) and ϱ ∈ [δ, 1] ∩ δ ηZ (here T ϱ means the dyadic ϱ-tube containing T ). In addition, the
quantity |TY (p)[T ϱ ]| is approximately the same independent of p ∈ U (T, Y ), and so |TY (p)|δ ∼ µ(T, Y ).
   For each p ∈ U (T, Y ), for ℓ = 1, . . . , d − 1, let δℓ be the smallest scale such that there exists an
ℓ-dimensional plane Σℓ through p such that
                                        |TY (p)[Nδℓ Σℓ ]|δ ⩾ δℓηℓ |TY (p)|δ .

point) and write δd = δ 1/2 .
  Therefore an ℓ-linear point p satisfies

   After pigeonholing, there exists ℓ ∈ [1, d] such that a ⪆ 1-fraction of points x ∈ U (T, Y ) is ℓ-

⪆ δℓηℓ δ η -dense shading on T.

   Suppose that ℓ = 1, then Nδ1 Σ1 is just a δ1 -tube. By item (1) and since TY (p) is ≈ 1-uniform,

     Now we prove the stronger version, Proposition 18.1, using Proposition 18.2.
Proof. By Proposition 18.2, for any δ0 , η0 > 0 we can find η ⩽ η0 and δ < δ0 such that there exists
a global (ℓ − 1)-grains (δ, η, κ)-Kakeya configuration whose set T of δ-tubes satisfies CCW (T) ⩽ δ −η .
The global (ℓ − 1)-grains (δ, η, κ)-Kakeya set is organized into a union of horizontal (ℓ − 1)-dimensional
slices. These slices themselves might be organized into a union of higher dimensional slices, this is
where the k-planar comes from.

                                        19. Properties of slope functions
   In this section we prove some useful basic properties of ℓ-linear k-planar Kakeya configurations.
First, we show that if either of the slope functions f or f˜ varies at a slower rate than Lipschitz, then
the set of tubes T violates Convex Wolff axioms. In the two proofs it is crucial that we use the Slab
Frostman and the Tube Katz–Tao axioms (respectively) included in Definition 17.4. The proof for f
will be similar to the proof of Lemma 12.5 of the corresponding Fustenberg statement, while the proof
for f˜ is based in part on [32, Lemma 6.3].
   The last property we need is that both functions f and f˜ may be assumed to be approximately
Lipschitz. In the next section we define ‘W -tuples’ – they were already used for sticky Fustenberg in
Section 12.1. Here the computations are largely the same, we include all details for readers convenience.

19.1. W -tuples. The contents of this section will be used in Sections 19.2 and 20.3.
  Fix an ℓ-linear k-planar (δ, η, κ, γ)-Kakeya configuration in Rd and a scale ϱ ∈ [δ 1/4 , 1). Fix a
dyadic ϱ-cube q = pϱ0 intersecting with E and write E(q) = E ∩ q, Z(q) as the set of z such that
Ez ∩ q ̸= ∅, Φq , and T(q) = ∪p∈E(q) Tp for the corresponding objects describing the structure of the
Kakeya configuration inside q. We can write E(q) in the form
                       E(q) = {(x, y + f (z)x, z) : z ∈ Z(q), y ∈ Yz (q), x ∈ Xy,z (q)}.
                      KAKEYA AND FURSTENBERG PROBLEMS FOR STICKY SETS OF TUBES                                                               77

We have the following properties that follow from Definition 17.2 (3), (4):
                                                         |E(q)| ∼δO(η) |Z(q)|(ϱ/δ)d−1−κ ,
                                                         |T(q)| ∼δO(η) ϱ−κ (ϱ/δ)d−1 ,
(146)                                     X
                                                     |Z(q, T )| ∼δO(η) |Z(q)| |T(q)|.
                                        T ∈T(q)


Definition of the W -tuples. For z, z ′ , z ′′ ∈ Z(q) define the set of W -tuples to be the following
Wz,z ′ ,z ′′ (q) = {(p, p1 , p2 , p′1 , p′2 , T1 , T2 , T1′ , T2′ ) ∈ Ez (q) × Ez ′ (q) × Ez ′ (q) × Ez ′′ (q) × Ez ′′ (q) × T(q)4 :
                                                                    Ti ∈ Tp ∩ Tpi ,               Ti′ ∈ Tpi ∩ Tp′i ,   |θ(T1′ ) − θ(T2′ )| ⩽ ϱ}
Thus a W -tuple is obtained by taking two copies of the same two-edge path from level z to level z ′′ ,
with a common starting point and ϱ-close tube directions. See Figure 1 for an illustration.
Counting the W tuples. The next lemma gives the expected lower bound.
Lemma 19.1. We have
                                        X
                                                    |Wz,z ′ ,z ′′ (q)| ≳δO(η) |E(q)|δ −4κ ϱκ |Z(q)|2 .
                                z,z ′ ,z ′′ ∈Z(q)

Proof. For z, z ′ , z ′′ ∈ Z(q), let
            Pz,z ′ ,z ′′ = {(p, p1 , p′1 , T1 , T1′ ) : p ∈ Ez (q), p1 ∈ Ez ′ (q), p′1 ∈ Ez ′′ (q), T1 , T1′ ∈ T(q)
                           T1 ∈ Tp ∩ Tp1 , T1′ ∈ Tp1 ∩ Tp′1 }.
So each W -tuple is composed of two 3-vertex paths in Pz,z ′ ,z ′′ starting at p. The estimates (146) give
                               X
(147)                                 |Pz,z ′ ,z ′′ | ∼δO(η) |E(q)|δ −2κ |Z(q)|2 .
                                     z,z ′ ,z ′′ ∈Z(q)

Indeed, first select p in |E(q)| ways, then T ∈ Tp ∩ T(q), p1 ∈ Z(T, q), T1′ ∈ Tp1 and p′1 ∈ Z(T1′ , q) can
be selected in ∼ δ −κ , ∼ |Z(q)| and ∼ δ −κ ways, respectively.
   Now for p ∈ Ez (q) and a dyadic cϱ-cube θ define a subset Pz,z ′ ,z ′′ (p, θ) ⊂ Pz,z ′ ,z ′′ consisting of tuples
(p, p1 , p′1 , T1 , T1′ ) with θ(T1′ ) ∈ θ. For every p, there are ∼δO(η) ϱ−κ -many choices for θ that make this
set non-empty. So using Cauchy–Schwarz it follows
                                                                                              2

  On the other hand, every element of Pz,z ′ ,z ′′ (p, θ)2 gives rise to a W -tuple and every W -tuple is
obtained this way in at most ∼ 1-many ways. This gives the desired formula for the number of
W -tuples.                                                                                             □

Additive relations. Fix a tuple in Wz,z ′ ,z ′′ (q) and write
                                                       p = (x, y + f (z)x, z),
                                                      pi = (xi , yi + f (z ′ )xi , z ′ ),
                                                      p′i = (x′i , yi′ + f (z ′′ )x′i , z ′′ ).
Let ξ = ξp , ξi = ξpi , and ξi′ = ξp′i . We denote the first ℓ − 1 direction coordinates of Ti and Ti′ by φi
and φ′i , respectively. In particular, |φ′1 − φ′2 | ⩽ ϱ.

     Since Ti passes through both p and pi , while Ti′ passes through both pi and p′i , Definition 17.2 gives
                          θ(Ti ) = (φi , ξ + f (z)φi ) + O(δ) = (φi , ξi + f (z ′ )φi ) + O(δ),
                          θ(Ti′ ) = (φ′i , ξi + f (z ′ )φ′i ) + O(δ) = (φ′i , ξi′ + f (z ′′ )φ′i ) + O(δ).

(148)              ξi = ξ + (f (z) − f (z ′ ))φi + O(δ),            ξi′ = ξi + (f (z ′ ) − f (z ′′ ))φ′i + O(δ),
and hence

(149)
     We have
                  pi = p + (z ′ − z)(θ(Ti ), 1) + O(δ),            p′i = pi + (z ′′ − z ′ )(θ(Ti′ ), 1) + O(δ).

19.2. Small variation of slope functions. In this section we show that if either of the slope functions
f and f˜ of a k-planar ℓ-linear Kakeya configuration varies slower than Lipschitz, then tubes in T
concentrate in convex sets.
Lemma 19.2. Let (E, T, {Tp }) be an ℓ-linear k-planar (δ, η, κ, γ)-Kakeya configuration in Rd with the
slope function f (z) ∈ Mat(d − ℓ, ℓ − 1) and η < γ. Suppose that for some ϱ ∈ [δ 1/3 , 1] and σ ∈ [ϱ2 , ϱ]
and every z0 ∈ Z, z = z0ϱ , we have
(154)                              #{z ∈ Z ∩ z : |f (z) − f (z0 )| ⩽ σ} ⩾ δ ε |Z ∩ z|.
Then we have CCW (T) ≳ δ O(η/γ+ε) (ϱ/σ)κ .
Proof. Fix p0 ∈ Ez0 and let q = pϱ0 and Z(q) = {z ∈ Z ∩ z : |f (z) − f (z0 )| ⩽ σ}.
  Write                                          
                                          f1 (z)

             U (z, T ) = Nσ T + [−ϱ, ϱ] · (1, 0, . . . , 0, f1 (z), 0) + [−ϱ, ϱ] · (0, 1, . . . , 0, f2 (z), 0) + · · ·
                                                                              +[−ϱ, ϱ] · (0, . . . , 0, 1, fℓ−1 (z), 0)

an appropriately large constant C and a typical choice of T ∈ T(q) and z ∈ Z(q). Consider the set of
W -tuples Wz,z ′ ,z ′′ (q) as defined in Section 19.1.
  For a pair of tubes T1′ , T2′ and z ′′ ∈ Z(q) we write (z ′′ , T1′ ) ∼ (z ′′ , T2′ ) if they can be extended to a
tuple (p, p′1 , p′2 , p′′1 , p′′2 , T1 , T2 , T1′ , T2′ ) ∈ Wz,z ′ ,z ′′ (q) for some z, z ′ ∈ Z(q).
Claim 19.3. There exists a constant C only depending on the dimension d such that if (z ′′ , T1′ ) ∼
(z ′′ , T2′ ) then U (z ′′ , T1′ ) ⊂ C · U (z ′′ , T2′ ) ⊂ C 2 · U (z ′′ , T1′ ).
Proof. Consider a tuple (p, p1 , p2 , p′1 , p′2 , T1 , T2 , T1′ , T2′ ) ∈ Wz,z ′ ,z ′′ (q) and write p = (x, y + f (z)x, z),
pi = (xi , yi + f (z ′ )xi , z ′ ) and p′i = (x′i , yi′ + f (z ′′ )x′i , z ′′ ). Let φ1 , φ2 , φ′1 , φ′2 denote the first ℓ − 1
coordinates of the slopes θ(T1 ), θ(T2 ), θ(T1′ ), θ(T2′ ). Then we have by (153) and |f (z ′ ) − f (z ′′ )| ⩽ σ for
any z ′ , z ′′ ∈ Z(q):
                   θ(T1′ ) − θ(T2′ ) = (φ′1 − φ′2 )(1, f (z ′ )) + (φ1 − φ2 )(0, f (z) − f (z ′ )) + O(δ)
                                        = (φ′1 − φ′2 )(1, f (z ′ )) + O(σ) = O(ϱ)(1, f (z ′′ )) + O(σ).
This means that even though θ(T1′ ) − θ(T2′ ) could be as large as O(ϱ), but the difference lies in
                      [−Cϱ, Cϱ] · (1, 0, . . . , 0, f1 (z ′′ )) + [−Cϱ, Cϱ] · (0, 1, . . . , 0, f2 (z ′′ ))+
                                               · · · + [−Cϱ, Cϱ] · (0, . . . , 0, 1, fℓ−1 (z ′′ )) + O(σ).
   By (150), (152):
                                    x′1 − x′2 = (z ′ − z)(φ1 − φ2 ) + O(ϱ2 ) = O(ϱ),
                                    y1′ − y2′ = O(ϱσ + δ).
Similarly, this says p′1 − p′2 lies in O(ϱσ + δ)-neighborhood of
                    [−Cϱ, Cϱ] · (1, 0, . . . , 0, f1 (z ′′ ), 0) + [−Cϱ, Cϱ] · (0, 1, . . . , 0, f2 (z ′′ ), 0)+
                                                          · · · + [−Cϱ, Cϱ] · (0, . . . , 0, 1, fℓ−1 (z ′′ ), 0).
   From this we conclude that p′2 ∈ U (z ′′ , T1′ ) and T2′ ⊂ C · U (z ′′ , T1′ ) which implies U (z ′′ , T1′ ) and
U (z ′′ , T2′ ) define essentially the same box, concluding the proof. Since the relation (z ′′ , T1′ ) ∼ (z ′′ , T2′ )
is symmetric, we also have C · U (z ′′ , T2′ ) ⊂ C 2 · U (z ′′ , T1′ ).                                            □

     Fix some (z, T ) ∈ P ′ and for an integer m ⩾ 1 consider the m-th neighborhood set Nm (z, T ) ⊂ P ′ in
G, i.e. this is the set of (z, T ′ ) ∈ P ′ which are connected to (z, T ) by a path with at most m edges (note
that the z-coordinate doesn’t change along the edges of G ′ ). By Claim 19.3 all pairs (z, T ′ ) ∈ Nm (z, T )
80                                        HONG WANG, DMITRII ZAKHAROV

are all contained in the same convex set Cm · U (z, T ). So it suffices to show that for a bounded m,
the neighborhood Nm (z, T ) is large.
   Now we observe that for (z ′′ , T1′ ) ∼ (z ′′ , T2′ ), if we denote by p′1 = (x′1 , y1′ + f (z ′′ )x′1 , z ′′ ) and p′2 =

did in (150), we get that
                                       x′1 − x′2 = (z ′ − z)(φ1 − φ2 ) + O(ϱ2 ).

Nm (z, T ) be arbitrary and let (z1 , z2 , φ1 , φ2 ) ∈ G(z, T ′ ). Then it follows from the above that there

                                x(z, T ′′ ) − x(z, T ′ ) = (z2 − z1 )(φ2 − φ1 ) + O(ϱ2 ).

graph

                                           |Gm | ⩾ δ O(η) |Xm ||Z(q)|2 |Φq |2 .

ing pair (z, Ti ) ∈ Nm (z, T ) and a corresponding point pi ∈ Ez ∩ Ti . We know that Ti ⊂ Cm · U (z, T ).
Furthermore, if we write θ(Ti ) ∈ Nδ (φi , ξpi + f (z)φi ) for some ξi ∈ Ξpi and φi ∈ Φpi , then using
AD-regularity of sets Φpi , we get ∼δO(η) (σ/ϱ)−κ -many σ-tubes T̃i ∈ Tσ which intersect the z-plane

(φ′i , ξpi + f (z)φ′i ) and let T̃i = (Ti′ )σ . For large enough constant K we get

Lemma 19.4. Let (E, T, {Tp }) be an ℓ-linear k-planar (δ, η, κ, γ)-Kakeya configuration in Rd with the
slope function f˜(z) ∈ Mat(d − k, k − 1). Suppose that for some ϱ ∈ [δ 1/3 , 1] and σ ∈ [ϱ2 , ϱ] and z0 ∈ Z,
z = z0ϱ , we have
                            #{z ∈ Z ∩ z : |f˜(z) − f˜(z0 )| ⩽ σ} ⩾ δ ε |Z ∩ z|.
Then we have CCW (T) ≳ δ C(ε+η)/γ (ϱ/σ)κ .
Proof. We write
                              E = {(x, y + f˜(z)x, z), x ∈ Xy,z , y ∈ Yz , z ∈ Z}
for sets Xy,z ⊂ [−1, 1]k−1 , Yz ⊂ [−1, 1]d−k and Z ⊂ [−1, 1]. In this proof we will not use the represen-
tation of E associated with the ℓ-linear slope function f .
   For z ∈ Z, y ∈ Yz and x ∈ Xy,z let ξ(x, y, z) ∈ [−1, 1]d−k be a vector such that for p = (x, y +
f˜(z)x, z) ∈ E and any T ∈ Tp we have θ(T ) ∈ Nδ (φ, ξ(x, y, z) + f˜(z)φ). The function ξ(x, y, z) is
constructed from ξp in Definition 17.2 (4) and the consistency relation in Definition 17.4 (1): every
T ∈ Tp satisfies
                              θ(T ) ∈ Nδ (φ1 , ξp + f (z)φ1 ), φ1 ∈ [−1, 1]ℓ−1 ,
            

                                                 = (φ, ξp,2 − f˜2 (z)ξp,1 + f˜(z)φ)
