From the dyadic Lipschitz property of ξp in Definition 17.2 (4) it follows that ξ(x, y, z) is ≈ δ −η

19.3. Slope functions are Lipschitz. In this section we show that after pigeonholing both slope
functions f and f˜ can be assumed to be dyadic Lipschitz. It is part of the data of Definition 17.2 (4)
that f is Lipschtitz, however we need to verify separately that so is f˜.
Lemma 19.5. Consider an ℓ-linear k-planar (δ, η, κ, γ)-Kakeya configuration and let f, f˜ be its slope
functions. Let ϱ ∈ [δ 1/3 , 1]. Then there exists Z̃ ⊂ Z with |Z̃| ≳ δ O(η) |Z| such that for every z0 ∈ Z̃
and z = z0ϱ we have for every z, z ′ ∈ Z̃ ∩ z that
                                             |f (z) − f (z ′ )| ⩽ δ −O(η/γ) ϱ
                                             |f˜(z) − f˜(z ′ )| ⩽ δ −O(η/γ) ϱ.

                  20. (d − 1)-linear configurations: Proof of Proposition 17.3
   In this section we consider (d − 1)-planar, (d − 1)-linear Kakeya configurations, which means k =
ℓ = d − 1 and f = f˜. We show that any such configuration must be full-dimensional.

20.2. Linear case. In the following lemma we view Mat(d − k, k − 1) as a subset in R(d−k)(k−1) and

k-planar k-linear (δ, η, κ, γ)-Kakeya configuration. This implies that the set Φq ⊂ (ϱZ)k ∩ [−1, 1]k is

δ O(η) δ O(η/γ) |r|. To see this, suppose the ℓ∞ -norm of r is achieved at the ij-th entry |r| = |rij | and
write λ = (λ1 , . . . , λk ) = φ1 − φ2 , then the set of λ such that | ℓ riℓ λℓ | ⩽ δ O(η/γ) |ri j| is contained in
                                                                       P

the δ O(η/γ) -neighborhood of a hyperplane. Since Φq is (ϱ, γ, δ −O(η) )-slab Frostman, for each φ1 , the
number of such φ2 is at most a δ O(η) -fraction.
   Thus, by double counting, we can fix values of φ1 , φ2 for which there are δ O(η) |Z(q)| choices for a
tuple (z, φ1 , φ2 ) satsifying (162) and which satisfy |r(φ1 −φ2 )| ≳ δ O(η/γ) |r|. Let v = r(φ1 −φ2 ) ∈ Rd−k .
Note that then we have
                          (z ′ − z)(f (z) − f (z ′ ))(φ1 − φ2 ) = (z ′ − z)2 v + O(ϱ2+ε0 |r|)
and so we conclude that for ∆ = Cϱ3 |r| + Cϱ2+ε0 |r| ∼ ϱ2+ε0 |r| we have
                          #{z ∈ Z(q) : y1 + (z ′ − z)2 v ∈ N∆ Yz ′ (q)} ≳δO(η) |Z(q)|

                                                                         ′
z ∈ Z(q) such that y1 + (z ′ − z)2 v ∈ N∆ Yz ′ (q) and |z − z ′ | ≳ δ ε+C η ϱ. By choosing large enough C ′ ,
from the above we get the lower bound |Z| ≳δO(η) |Z(q)|.
   Let Ty1 ,v ⊂ Rd−k be a C∆×Cϱ2 |r| tube centered at y1 and in direction v. Since we have |(z ′ −z)2 v| ⩽
Cϱ2 |r|, we conclude that we have the following lower bound on the covering number of Yz ′ (q) ∩ Ty1 ,v :

δ O(ε+η) ∆.

Now recall that ∆ ∼ ϱ2+ε0 |r| and so we conclude that γµε0 ⩽ O(ε + η/γ). By choosing ε, η sufficiently
small in ε0 , µ, γ we arrive at a contradiction.                                                    □

20.3. Non-linear case. In this section we show that there are no (d − 1)-linear Kakeya configurations
whose slope function cannot be locally approximated by a linear function.

depending on all previous parameters.

linear, so it suffices to deal with the non-linear case. Namely, in this section we prove the following.

appears in the linear case from the previous section.

Remark 21.3. Theorem 21.2 can be extended the case when A, B ⊂ [−1, 1]d for some d ⩾ 3. Indeed,
by taking a generic Rd → R2 projection π, one can show that π(B) still satisfies the two-ends tube
non-concentration condition (with slightly worse constants ε1 , ε2 and after mild pruning).
     Now we ready to prove the main result of this section.

                 22. 3-planar 2-linear in R4 case: proof of Proposition 17.5
  In this section we consider the 3-planar 2-linear Kakeya configurations, this is the last case of sticky
Kakeya in R4 . The overall strategy is the same as in the previous sections, except for the fact that
here we need to split into ‘quadratic vs non-quadratic’ cases instead of ‘linear vs non-linear’ as before.

22.2. Kaufman and radial projections in R3 . In the next section we will require 3-dimensional
versions of Theorem 13.2 and Theorem 13.4. For the radial projections result, we only state the version
for sets of points with dimension 2. This is the only case that we need and it can be done by a relatively
simple modification of the R2 argument.
Theorem 22.2. Let ε1 , ε2 , ζ ∈ (0, 1). Suppose that ε1 ⩽ ζ/100 .Then there exists δ0 = δ0 (ε1 , ε2 , ζ), η0 =
η0 (ε1 , ε2 , ζ) > 0 such that the following holds for all δ < δ0 , η < η0 . Let P ⊂ [0, 1]3 be a δ-separated
(δ, δ −η )-uniform (δ, 2, δ −η )-set such that |P ∩ S| ⩽ δ ε2 |P | for any δ ε1 × 1 × 1-slab S.
   Then there exists a subset G ⊂ P × P such that |G| ⩾ (1 − δ η/10 )|P |2 such that for every (p, p′ ) ∈ G
we have |Tp,p′ (ϱ) ∩ P | ⩽ δ −ζ ϱ2 |P | for all ϱ ∈ [δ, 1].
   In particular, for every p ∈ P such that |G|p | ⩾ ν|P |, the set of tubes {Tp,p′ (δ), (p, p′ ) ∈ G} is a
(δ, 2, Cδ −ζ ν −1 )-set.
     The proof is given in Appendix A.
Lemma 22.3. Let d ⩾ 2 and let ε > 0. The following holds for δ ⩽ δ0 and η < η0 (d, ε).
   Let P ⊂ [−1, 1]d be a δ-separated (δ, 1, δ −η )-set and let Λ ⊂ [−1, 1]d−1 be a δ-separated (δ, d − 1, δ −η )-
set. Then there exists Λ′ ⊂ Λ, |Λ′ | ⩾ (1 − δ η )|Λ|, and Pλ ⊂ P for each λ ∈ Λ′ , |Pλ | ⩾ (1 − δ η )|P | such
that πλ (Pλ ) is a (δ, 1, δ −ε )-set. Here πλ : Rd → R is the linear projection (x1 , . . . , xd ) → λ1 x1 + . . . +
λd−1 xd−1 + xd .
Proof. The proof is identical to the proof of Theorem 13.4, so we skip it.                                              □

22.3. Proof of Proposition 17.5. Let (T, E, {Tp }) be a 3-planar 2-linear (δ, η, κ, γ)-Kakeya config-
uration such that CCW (T) ≲ δ −η . Note that both sets Yz and Φp are subsets in [−1, 1]. This means
that the γ parameter does not play a role in the definition of E (we can redefine γ = 1). Let
                                 
                           f1 (z)
                  f (z) =           ∈ Mat(2, 1), f˜(z) = (f˜1 (z), f˜2 (z)) ∈ Mat(1, 2)
                           f2 (z)

   Let ϱ ∈ [δ 1/4 , δ µ ] be an intermediate scale. We consider several cases depending on how f and f˜
behave on ϱ-intervals. First, by Lemma 19.5, we can pass to a subset in Z of density δ O(η/κ) so that
f and f˜ are δ −η -Lischitz on scale ϱ.
   By Lemma 21.1, we either get κ ⩽ ζ or there is some ε0 = ε0 (κ) > 0 and a subset Z ′ ⊂ Z of density
            ′
at least δ η such that for every z0 ∈ Z ′ and z = z0ϱ there exists a vector rz = r = (r1 , r2 ) ∈ R × R so
that
(184)                                    |f (z) − f (z ′ ) − (z − z ′ )r| ⩽ ϱ1+ε0
holds for all z, z ′ ∈ Z ′ ∩ z. Here we take η ′ to be the ε from the Lemma 21.1 and assume that η is
sufficiently small in η ′ , ζ, µ. Furthermore, by Lemma 19.2 and Lemma 19.5 we may assume that

(185)                                           |r| ∈ [δ Oκ (η) , δ −Oκ (η) ].
                       KAKEYA AND FURSTENBERG PROBLEMS FOR STICKY SETS OF TUBES                                                97


  For z0 ∈ Z and z ∈ z = z0ϱ define fz0 (z) = f2 (z) − f˜2 (z0 )f1 (z) and f˜z0 (z ′ ) = f˜2 (z ′ ) − f˜2 (z0 ). Consider
the expression in (179), we now can write
                                 f2 (z) − f2 (z ′ ) − f˜2 (z ′ )(f1 (z) − f1 (z ′ ))
                                =fz (z) − fz (z ′ ) − (f˜2 (z ′ ) − f˜2 (z0 ))(f1 (z) − f1 (z ′ ))
                                    0               0

                                =fz0 (z) − fz0 (z ′ ) − f˜z0 (z ′ )r1 (z − z ′ ) + O(δ −η ϱ2+ε0 ).

Here we used the Lipschitz property |f˜z0 (z ′ )| = |f˜2 (z ′ )−f˜2 (z0 )| = O(δ −η ϱ) and the linear approximation
of f1 . Note that after a change of coordinates we may assume that f (z0 ) = 0 and f˜(z0 ) = 0. In these
coordinates we then have fz0 (z) = f2 (z) and f˜z0 (z ′ ) = f˜2 (z ′ ). So one should think of functions fz0 and
f˜z0 as f2 and f˜2 written in the coordinates ‘axis-aligned’ at the level z0 .
    We split the proof into two cases depending on whether fz0 (z) can be well approximated by a
quadratic polynomial. By combining Lemma 22.4 and Lemma 22.5 stated in the following sections, we
conclude that κ ⩽ ζ for any ζ > 0, provided that η is small enough.
    More precisely, we choose parameters as follows. Let ε = ε(ζ) be given by Lemma 22.4. Let ε1 = ε
and let ε2 = ε2 (ζ, ε1 ) be given by Lemma 22.5. Let η̃ = min(ε2 , η̃(ε)) where η̃(ε) is given by Lemma
22.4. Then if η is small enough in ε and κ > ζ, then by Lemma 22.4 we conclude that fz0 (z) cannot be
approximated by a quadratic polynomial a0 + a1 z + a2 z 2 with error ϱε (ϱ2 + ϱ|a1 | + ϱ2 |a2 |) for a δ η̃ -dense
subset of z ∈ Z. This then means that the non-concentration condition in Lemma 22.5 is satisfied with
ε1 = ε and ε2 = η̃. So for η small enough in ε, η̃, µ we conclude that κ ⩽ ζ. This completes the proof
of Proposition 17.5 and so it remains to prove the two lemmas.

22.4. Quadratic case. The first case we consider is when fz0 (z) can be well approximated by a
quadratic polynomial on each ϱ-interval.
Lemma 22.4. Let η̃, ε > 0 and Z̃ ⊂ Z be a subset of density at least δ η̃ . Suppose that for every z0 ∈ Z̃
and z = z0ϱ there are coefficients a0 , a1 , a2 ∈ R such that
                      fz0 (z) = a0 + a1 (z − z0 ) + a2 (z − z0 )2 + O(ϱε (ϱ2 + ϱ|a1 | + ϱ2 |a2 |))
holds for all z ∈ Z̃ ∩ z.
  Then κ ⩽ ζ, provided that ε is small enough in ζ and η, η̃ is small enough in ε.
Proof. Without loss of generality assume that ε ⩽ ε0 /2 for the ε0 in (184) and η is small enough in ε0
and µ.

22.5. Non-quadratic case. Now we consider the case when fz0 cannot be approximated by a qua-
dratic polynomial.
Lemma 22.5. Let ε1 , ε2 ∈ (0, ε0 /2) and suppose that for every z0 ∈ Z, z = z0ϱ and any coefficients

                              Appendix A. Radial projection theorems
   In this appendix we prove the radial projection theorems used in our proofs. The arguments here
broadly follow the proof steps in the Orponen–Shmerkin–Wang paper [22] though details are quite
different.
A.1. Radial projections in 2D. We repeat the statement of Theorem 13.2 here for the readers
convenience.
Theorem A.1. Let t ∈ (0, 2] and ε1 , ε2 , ζ ∈ (0, 1). Suppose that ε1 ⩽ ζ/8. Then there exist δ0 =
δ0 (t, ε1 , ε2 , ζ) > 0 and η0 = η0 (t, ε1 , ζ) > 0 such that the following holds for all δ < δ0 and η < η0 . Let
P ⊂ [0, 1]2 be a δ-separated (δ, δ −η )-uniform (δ, t, δ −η )-set such that |P ∩T | ⩽ δ ε2 |P | for any δ ε1 ×1-tube
T.
    Then there exists a subset G ⊂ P × P such that |G| ⩾ (1 − δ min(η,ε2 /2) )|P |2 such that for every
(p, p′ ) ∈ G we have |Tp,p′ (ϱ) ∩ P | ⩽ δ −ζ ϱmin(1,t) |P | for all ϱ ∈ [δ, 1].
    In particular, for every p ∈ P such that |G|p | ⩾ ν|P |, the set of tubes {Tp,p′ (δ), (p, p′ ) ∈ G} is a
(δ, min(1, t), Cδ −ζ ν −1 )-set.
  The proof of the following lemma is a mild modification of arguments in [18, Section 2] and [26,
Appendix B].
Lemma A.2. Let t ∈ (0, 2] and ε1 , ε2 > 0. There is an absolute constant c > 0 such that the following
holds whenever 0 < χ < ctε1 and δ < δ0 (t, ε1 , ε2 ).
   Let P ⊂ [−1, 1]2 be a (δ, t, δ −χ )-set such that |P ∩ T | ⩽ δ ε2 |P | for every δ ε1 × 1-tube T . Then
there exists G ⊂ P × P such that |G| ⩾ (1 − δ min(ε1 t/2,ε2 /2) )|P |2 and |Tp,p′ (δ 4ε1 ) ∩ P | ⩽ δ χ |P | for all
(p, p′ ) ∈ G.

A.2. Radial projections in 3D. We now adapt the proof of Theorem 13.2 to prove Theorem 22.2.
We restate it here for convenience.
Theorem A.4. Let ε1 , ε2 , ζ ∈ (0, 1). Suppose that ε1 ⩽ ζ/100. Then there exist δ0 = δ0 (ε1 , ε2 , ζ) > 0
and η0 = η0 (ε1 , ε2 , ζ) > 0 such that the following holds for all δ < δ0 and η < η0 . Let P ⊂ [0, 1]3 be a
δ-separated (δ, δ −η )-uniform (δ, 2, δ −η )-set such that |P ∩ S| ⩽ δ ε2 |P | for any δ ε1 × 1 × 1-slab S.
   Then there exists a subset G ⊂ P × P such that |G| ⩾ (1 − δ η/10 )|P |2 such that for every (p, p′ ) ∈ G
we have |Tp,p′ (ϱ) ∩ P | ⩽ δ −ζ ϱ2 |P | for all ϱ ∈ [δ, 1].
   In particular, for every p ∈ P such that |G|p | ⩾ ν|P |, the set of tubes {Tp,p′ (δ), (p, p′ ) ∈ G} is a
(δ, 2, Cδ −ζ ν −1 )-set.
Remark A.5. We state the result only for 2-dimensional sets P since it suffices for our applications
and allows for a simpler (although somewhat ad hoc) proof. A general sharp radial projections theorem
in all dimensions was proven by Ren [24]. However, the statements of Ren’s results do not suffice for
our applications although it is plausible that his techniques can be modified to prove an appropriate
generalization of Theorem A.4.
   Note that unlike Theorem A.1, in this statement the parameter η0 is allowed to depend on ε2 . It
might be possible to remove this dependence via some generalization of Lemma A.2 but it seems to
require significant modifications to the proof strategy below and so we do not pursue it here.

Let us briefly outline the main steps needed to justify this. First, consider the maximal family T
of essentially distinct τ -tubes which contain ≳ ντ -fraction of P . By double counting, we show that
|T| = (λν)O(1) τ −2 . By Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, there are (λν)O(1) |T|2 many pairs of tubes T, T ′ ∈ T
that have non-empty intersection. Let θ be the typical angle between a pair of intersecting tubes in
T. Then T splits into essentially disjoint families T[Tθ ] for some collection of ∼ θ-tubes Tθ . By using a
two-ends argument on the subfamily T[Tθ ], we then conclude that |P ∩ Tθ | ≳ (λν)O(1) |P |. On the other
hand the Frostman property of P implies that |P ∩ Tθ | ≲ δ −η θ|P |. So it follows that θ ≳δO(1) (λν)O(1) ,
i.e. the tubes in T intersect roughly transversally. Now the classical Wolff hairbrush argument shows
that a large fraction of tubes in T must be contained in a ≈ τ slab Sτ , giving (253). We refer to [16,
Lemma 3.5] for a rigorous argument proving a closely related statement.
   Now we use (253) to reach a contradiction. Recall that by assumption, λ ⩾ δ O(η) . Choose ε∗2
sufficiently small, and then η sufficiently small, so that (251) and (253) give
                                 width(Sτ ) ⩽ δ ε1 ,            |P ∩ Sτ | > δ ε2 |P |.
                      ∗                                                                  ∗
Indeed, by (248), ∆ε1 = δ (1+κ0 )ε1 , leaving room to absorb the factors ∆−Cε2 δ −Cη in the slab width.
This contradicts the hypothesis of the theorem and proves the claim.

pair (p, p′ ) ∈ G2 \ G2 is contained in at most ≲ r−1 ϱ−1 -many slabs S ∈ S, we obtain
                       X              X

  Dyadically pigeonhole the set of slabs S so that |G4 [S]| and |P ∩S∆ | are roughly P

Let K = δ −C1 η for a sufficiently large constant C1 and let PS ⊂ P ∩ S∆ be a (∆, 1, K)-set contained
in P ∩ S∆ of the maximum possible size. We claim that for sufficiently large C1 , we have the estimate
|PS | ⩾ (1 − K −1/2 )|P ∩ S∆ |. Indeed, for every dyadic scale w ∈ [∆, 1] consider the set Qw,j of finitely
overlapping w-cubes q satisfying |P ∩ S∆ ∩ q| ∼ 2j w|P ∩ S∆ |. By definition, we have
                                    I1∆ (µS ) ≳ |Qw,j |w−1 (2j w|P ∩ S∆ |)2
and so the upper bound on Riesz energy gives |Qw,j | ≲ δ −O(η) 2−2j w−1 and |

δ −O(η) 2−j |P ∩ S∆ |.
                     Summing this over all dyadic w and all j ⩾ log2 K, we get that the union of all
rich cubes covers at most δ −O(η) K −1 fraction of points of P ∩ S∆ . For C1 large enough this fraction is
less than K −1/2 and removing it from the set leaves a (∆, 1, K)-set PS .

  Observe that

Step 4: reduction to the planar argument. Let GS = G4 [S] ∩ (PS × PS ). We conclude that S ∈ S and
S∆ = N∆ S have the following properties:
     (i) The set PS is (∆, 1, K)-set,
    (ii) |GS | ≳ δ O(η) |P ∩ S∆ |2 ≳ δ O(η) |PS |2 ,
   (iii) every (p, p′ ) ∈ GS satisfies
                                       |Tp,p′ (ϱ) ∩ P ∩ S∆ | ⩾ δ −ζ/2 ϱ2 |P |
      (iv) every (p, p′ ) ∈ GS satisfies the two-ends condition
                                                 ∗                       ∗
                                     |Tp,p′ (∆ε1 ) ∩ P ∩ S∆ | ⩽ ∆ε2 |P ∩ S∆ |.
  We want to use properties (i)–(iv) to reduce to the situation to the one in the proof of Theorem A.1.
To achieve this, we need to strengthen condition (iii). First, we estimate M ∼ |P ∩ S∆ | from above.

By rearranging we get the upper bound |T| ≲δO(η) δ ζ/3 ϱ−2 . Thus, on average, a tube T ∈ T contains

for every (p, p′ ) ∈ GS .
   We can now repeat the proof of Theorem A.1 starting from Step 1 using the graph GS in place of
G1 (after projecting everything onto the plane parallel to S). More precisely, we have the following
matching between parameters in the current setup and Theorem A.1:
   The restriction ε1 ⩽ ζ/100 in the statement of Theorem A.4 implies that ε∗1 ⩽ (ζ/8β)/8 holds. So
for small enough η and δ, the parameters above satisfy the setup of Theorem A.1 and so its proof
